<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:metric-speed}
A curve $\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{X}$ is absolutely continuous if there exists a nonnegative function $g\in L^1(0,1)$ such that
\[
\mathrm{d}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))
\leq\int_s^t g(u)\,\mathrm d u,
\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq1.
\]
For such a curve, by \cite[Theorem~1.1.2]{ambrosio2005gradient}, the ratio $\mathrm{d}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))/|t-s|$, with $s\in[0,1]\setminus\{t\}$, has a limit as $s\to t$ for almost every $t\in(0,1)$ with respect to Lebesgue measure. We denote this limit by $|\Dot\gamma|(t)$ and call it the metric speed of $\gamma$ at $t$. Moreover, $|\Dot\gamma|\in L^1(0,1)$ and
\[
\mathrm{d}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))
\leq\int_s^t |\Dot\gamma|(u)\,\mathrm d u,
\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq1.
\]
For $x,y\in\mathcal{X}$, we denote by $A(x,y)$ the set of all absolutely continuous curves $\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{X}$ with $\gamma(0)=x$ and $\gamma(1)=y$. In particular, $\Gamma(x,y)\subseteq A(x,y)$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:CAT}
	Let $(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d})$ be a metric space and $\kappa\in\mathbb R$.
    We say that $(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d})$ has {\it global curvature bounded from above by $\kappa$} if and only if for all triangles $\Delta\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ with $\mathrm{per}(\Delta)<2D_\kappa$ and for all $x,y\in \Delta$, $\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leq \mathrm{d}_\kappa(\bar x,\bar y)$, where $\bar x$ and $\bar y$ are the points on a comparison triangle $\bar\Delta$ in $M_\kappa$ that correspond to $x$ and $y$ respectively.
    We say that {\it $(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d})$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space}
    if it is a geodesic space, complete, and has global curvature bounded from above by $\kappa$ (note that some authors do not require completeness in the definition of $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ spaces, but we do).
    We say that $(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d})$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}$ space if it is a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
A {\it $\kappa$-domain} is
any closed ball $B(o, r)$ with $r <\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$ if $\kappa>0$, and any ball or $\mathcal{X}$ itself if $\kappa\leq 0$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\cite[Proposition II.1.4]{bridsonhaefliger1999}
\label{prop:unique-geodesics-domain}
    Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Then, between any two points belonging to some $\kappa$-domain, there is a unique geodesic, and this geodesic does not leave the $\kappa$-domain.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:comparison_angles} Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Let $x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}$ with $x\notin\{y,z\}$ satisfying $\mathrm d(x,y)+\mathrm d(y,z)+\mathrm d(x,z)<2D_\kappa$.
The $\kappa$-comparison angle $\angle_x^{(\kappa)}(y, z)$ is the angle at $\bar x$ in the comparison triangle $(\bar x,\bar y,\bar z)$ in $M_\kappa$. That is, by the spherical/hyperbolic law of cosines \cite[I.2.2 and I.2.7]{bridsonhaefliger1999},
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
& \angle_x^{(\kappa)}(y,z) := \\
    & \qquad  
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\begin{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \arccos\left(
    \dfrac{
        \cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(y,z)\bigr)
        -\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
         \cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
    }{
        \sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
        \sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
    }
    \right)
    & \text{if } \kappa>0,
    \\[1.2em]
    \arccos\left(
    \dfrac{
        \mathrm{d}(x,y)^2+\mathrm{d}(x,z)^2-\mathrm{d}(y,z)^2
    }{
        2\mathrm{d}(x,y)\mathrm{d}(x,z)
    }
    \right)
    & \text{if } \kappa=0,
    \\[1.2em]
    \arccos\left(
    \dfrac{
        \cosh\bigl(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
        \cosh\bigl(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
        -\cosh\bigl(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(y,z)\bigr)
    }{
        \sinh\bigl(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
        \sinh\bigl(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
    }
    \right)
    & \text{if } \kappa<0.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\end{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  



In particular, $\angle_x^{(\kappa)}(y,y)=0$ whenever $y\neq x$ and the perimeter condition holds. One can easily check that the ratios inside the $\arccos$ are indeed always in $[-1,1]$, thanks to the triangle inequality and the perimeter assumption.
Note as well that,
for fixed distinct points $x,y,z$, the map $\kappa\mapsto\angle_x^{(\kappa)}(y,z)$ is continuous on the set of parameters satisfying the perimeter condition.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:euclidean-angle}
 When $\kappa=0$, $\angle_x^{(0)}(y,z)$ is called the Euclidean comparison angle, and it is defined in any metric space provided $x\notin\{y,z\}$, without a
perimeter restriction.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:alexandrov-angle}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. For two non-constant geodesics $\gamma,\eta$ issuing from $x\in\mathcal{X}$, their Alexandrov angle at $x$ is defined by
\[
\angle_x(\gamma,\eta)
:=
\lim_{s,t\downarrow0}
\angle_x^{(0)}(\gamma(s),\eta(t)).
\]
The limit exists by the $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ property.

For three distinct points $x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}$ and, if $\kappa>0$, assuming $\mathrm{d}(x,y),\mathrm{d}(x,z)<D_\kappa$, let $\gamma\in\Gamma(x,y)$ and $\eta\in\Gamma(x,z)$ be the unique geodesics. We define
\[
\angle_x(y,z):=\angle_x(\gamma,\eta).
\]
We also set $\angle_x(y,y):=0$ whenever $y\neq x$ and the geodesic from $x$ to $y$ is unique.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:angle-comparison}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$. Let $x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}$. Assume that $x\notin\{y,z\}$ and that $\mathrm d(x,y)+\mathrm d(y,z)+\mathrm d(x,z)<2D_\kappa$. Then,
\[
\angle_x^{(\kappa)}(y,z)\geq\angle_x(y,z).
\]
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:cosine-comparison-domain}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$, and let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain. For $x,y,z\in D$, with $x\notin\{y,z\}$,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:cosine-angle-comparison}
\frac{
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(y,z)\bigr)
-
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
}{
\sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)
\sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(x,z)\bigr)
}
\leq \cos\angle_x(y,z).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:geodesic-cosine-inequality}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$, and let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain. Let $x,y,z\in D$ and $\gamma\in\Gamma(y,z)$.
Set
\[
\alpha_t:=
\frac{\sin((1-t)\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(y,z))}
     {\sin(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(y,z))},
\qquad
\beta_t:=
\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(y,z))}
     {\sin(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(y,z))}.
\]
When $y=z$, these coefficients are understood as
$\alpha_t=1-t$ and $\beta_t=t$.
Then, for every $t\in[0,1]$,
\[
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,\gamma(t))\bigr)
\geq
\alpha_t\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,y)\bigr)
+
\beta_t\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr).
\]
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def:tangent-cone}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and let $x\in \mathcal{X}$. Let $\Gamma_x=\bigcup_{y\in \mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\}}\Gamma(x,y)$ be the set of all non-constant geodesics issuing from $x$. We call any two pairs $(s,\gamma),(t,\eta)\in [0,\infty)\times \Gamma_x$ equivalent if and only if, ($s=t=0$) or ($s=t>0$ and $\angle_x(\gamma,\eta)=0$. We denote by $\tilde C_x\mathcal{X}$ the quotient space. For $a\geq0$ and $\gamma\in\Gamma_x$, we write $a[\gamma]$ for the equivalence class of $(a,\gamma)$ and if $a=0$, we simply write it as $0_x$, which is the vertex of the cone $\tilde C_x\mathcal{X}$. We equip $\tilde C_x\mathcal{X}$ with the distance 
\[
\mathrm{d}_{\widetilde C_x\mathcal{X}}(s[\gamma],t[\eta])
:=
\left(
s^2+t^2-2st\cos\angle_x(\gamma,\eta)
\right)^{1/2},
\]
which does not depend on the choice of $\gamma$ and $\eta$ in the equivalence classes.

The tangent cone $C_x\mathcal{X}$ is the metric completion of $\widetilde C_x\mathcal{X}$, and its distance is denoted by $\mathrm{d}_{C_x\mathcal{X}}$. If there are no non-constant geodesic issuing from $x$, we simply set $C_x\mathcal{X}:=\{0_x\}$.

For $v,w\in C_x\mathcal{X}$, we define
\[
|v|_x:=\mathrm{d}_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(v,0_x),
\qquad
\langle v,w\rangle_x
:=
\frac12\left(
|v|_x^2+|w|_x^2-\mathrm{d}_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(v,w)^2
\right).
\]
In particular, for all $s,t\geq0$ and $\gamma,\eta\in\Gamma_x$,
\[
|s[\gamma]|_x=s,
\qquad
\langle s[\gamma],t[\eta]\rangle_x
=st\cos\angle_x(\gamma,\eta).
\]
For every $\lambda\geq0$, the map $s[\gamma]\mapsto(\lambda s)[\gamma]$ extends uniquely and continuously to $C_x\mathcal{X}$. We denote its value at $v$
by $\lambda v$. It satisfies $\mathrm{d}_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(\lambda v,\lambda w) =\lambda\mathrm{d}_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(v,w)$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:tangent-cone-cat-zero}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Then, for every $x\in\mathcal{X}$, the tangent cone $C_x\mathcal{X}$ is a complete $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:first-variation}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain. Let $x,y,z\in D$ with $x\neq y$ and $x\neq z$ and let $\gamma$ be the unique geodesic from $x$ to $z$. Then
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:first-variation}
\lim_{t\downarrow0}
\frac{\mathrm d(\gamma(t),y)-\mathrm d(x,y)}{t}
=-\mathrm d(x,z)\cos\angle_x(z,y)
=-\frac{\langle\log_x y,\log_x z\rangle_x}{\mathrm d(x,y)}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:projection}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain of $\mathcal{X}$, and let $C\subseteq D$ be a nonempty, closed,
geodesically convex set. Let $x\in\mathcal{X}$ satisfy
\[
\mathrm{d}(x,C):=\inf_{z\in C}\mathrm{d}(x,z)<D_\kappa/2.
\]
Recall that $D_\kappa=+\infty$ when $\kappa\leq0$, so this restriction is then automatic. There exists a unique point $p_C(x)\in C$ such that
\[
\mathrm{d}(x,p_C(x))=\mathrm{d}(x,C).
\]
Furthermore, if $x\notin C$, then
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eqn:obtuse}
\angle_{p_C(x)}(x,c)\geq\pi/2
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
for every $c\in C\setminus\{p_C(x)\}$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:Reshetnyak}
A complete geodesic space $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space if and only if,
for all $x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4\in\mathcal{X}$,
\[
\left|
\mathrm d^2(x_1,x_3)-\mathrm d^2(x_2,x_3)
-\mathrm d^2(x_1,x_4)+\mathrm d^2(x_2,x_4)
\right|
\leq
2\,\mathrm d(x_1,x_2)\mathrm d(x_3,x_4).
\]
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:euclidean-cone}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a metric space. Consider the following equivalence relation on $[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}$: for all $s,t\geq0$ and $x,y\in\mathcal X$, $(t,x)\sim(s,y)$ if and only if
$t=s=0$, or $t=s>0$ and $x=y$. The quotient space is called the Euclidean cone over $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ and is denoted by
$C_0\mathcal X$.

For every $(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{X}$, we write its equivalence class as $tx$. If $t=0$, we simply write it as $0$ and call it the vertex of $C_0\mathcal X$. Finally, for every $\kappa>0$, setting
\[
\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^{(\kappa)}(tx,sy)
:=
\left(
t^2+s^2
-2st\cos\bigl(\min\{\pi,\sqrt\kappa\mathrm{d}(x,y)\}\bigr)
\right)^{1/2}
\]
for all $x,y\in\mathcal{X}$ and $s,t\geq0$ defines a metric on $C_0\mathcal{X}$ by \cite[Proposition~I.5.9(1)]{bridsonhaefliger1999}.
For $\kappa=1$, we simply write $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}$. Finally, for $A\subseteq\mathcal{X}$, we identify $C_0A$ with a subset of $C_0\mathcal{X}$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:local-four-point}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$.
Let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain of $\mathcal{X}$.
Then, for every $x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4\in D$,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eq:squared-four-point}
\big|\mathrm{d}^2(x_1,x_3)-\mathrm{d}^2(x_2,x_3)-\mathrm{d}^2(x_1,x_4)+\mathrm{d}^2(x_2,x_4)\big|\leq c_{4\bullet}(r\sqrt\kappa)\,\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2)\mathrm{d}(x_3,x_4),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
where $r\in(0,\pi/(2\sqrt{\kappa}))$ is the radius of $D$ and, for all $u\in [0,\pi/2)$,

    {

    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:Q-constant}
c_{4\bullet}(u):=
\frac{4u}{\sin(2u)}
+
2m(u)^2
\frac{
\sin(2u)
-2u\cos(2u)
}{
\sin^3(2u)
},
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
with
\[
m(u)
:=
\max_{0\leq s\leq2u}\sin s
=
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\begin{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\sin(2u),
&0\leq u\leq\pi/4,\\
1,
&\pi/4<u<\pi/2.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {

    {

\]
The constant $c_{4\bullet}(u)$ is understood as $2$ when $u=0$.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$.

Write $D=B(o,r)$ for some $o\in\mathcal{X}$ and $r\in (0,\pi/2)$.
Note that by the triangle inequality, for all $x,y\in D$, we have $\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leq 2r<\pi$. 

Fix $x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4\in D$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 1: A cosine four-point inequality.}
Consider the Euclidean cone $C_0\mathcal{X}$, equipped with its
canonical distance $d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}$.
By Berestovskii's cone theorem,
\cite[Theorem~II.3.14]{bridsonhaefliger1999},
this cone is $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$.
For $i=1,\ldots,4$, set $\bar x_i:=1x_i\in C_0\mathcal{X}$.
Since $\mathrm{d}(x_i,x_j)<\pi$,
\[
d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(\bar x_i,\bar x_j)
=2\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm{d}(x_i,x_j)\bigr),
\qquad
d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\bar x_i,\bar x_j)
\leq\mathrm{d}(x_i,x_j).
\]
Applying Proposition~\ref{prop:Reshetnyak} in $C_0\mathcal{X}$ yields
\[
\Big|d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(\bar x_1,\bar x_3)-d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(\bar x_2,\bar x_3)-d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(\bar x_1,\bar x_4)+d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(\bar x_2,\bar x_4)\Big| \leq 2d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\bar x_1,\bar x_2)d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\bar x_3,\bar x_4).
\]
Writing $a_{ij}:=1-\cos\mathrm{d}(x_i,x_j)$ for $i,j=1,\ldots,4$ and dividing by $2$,
we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:appendix-cosine-four-point}
|a_{13}-a_{23}-a_{14}+a_{24}|
\leq\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2)\mathrm{d}(x_3,x_4).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 2: A scalar composition estimate.}
Let $H:I\to \mathbb R$ be a $C^2$ function on an non-empty interval $I$ and let
$L:=\max_I |H'|$ and $M:=\max_I|H''|$.
For $a,b\in I$, define
\[
J(a,b):=\int_0^1 H'((1-t)b+ta)\,\mathrm d t
\]
so for all $a,b,c,d\in I$, $H(a)-H(b)=(a-b)J(a,b)$, $|J(a,b)|\leq L$, and
\[
|J(a,b)-J(c,d)|
\leq\frac M2\bigl(|a-c|+|b-d|\bigr).
\]
Consequently, using
\[
H(a)-H(b)-H(c)+H(d)
=(a-b-c+d)J(a,b)+(c-d)\bigl(J(a,b)-J(c,d)\bigr),
\]
we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:scalar-composition}
|H(a)-H(b)-H(c)+H(d)|\leq L|a-b-c+d|+\frac M2|c-d|
\bigl(|a-c|+|b-d|\bigr).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 3: Transfer to squared distances.}
Take $I=[0,1-\cos(2r)]$ and 
\[
H(u):=\bigl(\arccos(1-u)\bigr)^2,
\qquad u\in[0,1-\cos(2r)].
\]
Thus $H(1-\cos s)=s^2$ for $s\in[0,2r]$.
For $s=\arccos(1-u)>0$,
\[
H'(u)=\frac{2s}{\sin s},
\qquad
H''(u)=2\frac{\sin s-s\cos s}{\sin^3s}.
\]
These derivatives extend continuously to $u=0$,
with $H'(0)=2$ and $H''(0)=2/3$.
Moreover,
\[
|H'|\leq L_r:=\frac{4r}{\sin(2r)},
\qquad
|H''|\leq M_r:=
2\frac{\sin(2r)-2r\cos(2r)}{\sin^3(2r)}.
\]
Indeed, $s/\sin s$ is increasing on $[0,\pi)$.
The function $f(s):=(\sin s-s\cos s)/\sin^3s$ is also
increasing, since
\[
f'(s)=\frac{g(s)}{\sin^4s},
\qquad
g(s):=s(1+2\cos^2s)-3\sin s\cos s,
\]
and
\[
g(0)=0,\qquad
g'(s)=4\sin s(\sin s-s\cos s)\geq0.
\]

Set $m:=m(r)=\max_{0\leq s\leq2r}\sin s$.
Since $s\mapsto1-\cos s$ is $m$-Lipschitz on $[0,2r]$,
the reverse triangle inequality gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
|a_{14}-a_{24}|&\leq m\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2),\\
|a_{13}-a_{14}|&\leq m\mathrm{d}(x_3,x_4),\\
|a_{23}-a_{24}|&\leq m\mathrm{d}(x_3,x_4).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:scalar-composition} to
$(a,b,c,d)=(a_{13},a_{23},a_{14},a_{24})$,
and using \eqref{eq:appendix-cosine-four-point}, yields
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\Big|
&\mathrm{d}^2(x_1,x_3)-\mathrm{d}^2(x_2,x_3)
-\mathrm{d}^2(x_1,x_4)+\mathrm{d}^2(x_2,x_4)
\Big|\\
&\leq
\bigl(L_r+M_rm^2\bigr)
\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2)\mathrm{d}(x_3,x_4),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
proving the normalized case since $L_r+M_rm^2=c_{4\bullet}(r)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{eq:L-convexity}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$, and let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain.
For $x,y,z\in D$, let $\gamma\in\Gamma(x,y)$ and $\eta\in\Gamma(x,z)$.
Then, for every $t\in[0,1]$,
\[
\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))
\leq
\frac{2r\sqrt\kappa}
     {\sin(2r\sqrt\kappa)}
\,t\,\mathrm{d}(y,z)
\]
where $r\in[0,\pi/(2\sqrt{\kappa}))$ is the radius of $D$. The quotient is understood as $1$ when $r=0$.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:weighted-distance}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa>0$, and let $D=B(o,r)$ be a $\kappa$-domain, with
$0\leq r<\pi/(2\sqrt{\kappa})$.
For $x,y\in D$, define
\[
\mathrm{d}_o(x,y):=
\inf_{\substack{\gamma\in A(x,y)\\\gamma([0,1])\subseteq D}}
\int_0^1
\frac{|\dot\gamma|(s)}
     {\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}(o,\gamma(s))\bigr)}
\,\mathrm d s,
\]
where $A(x,y)$ is defined in
Definition~\ref{def:metric-speed}, and
$|\dot\gamma|$ denotes the metric speed from
Definition~\ref{def:metric-speed}.
Then using \cite[Theorem~1.1.2]{ambrosio2005gradient}, $\mathrm{d}_o$ defines a distance on $D$ satisfying
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:equivalence-inequality}
\mathrm{d}(x,y)
\leq \mathrm{d}_o(x,y)
\leq \frac{1}{\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)}\mathrm{d}(x,y)
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
for all $x,y\in D$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:endpoint-inequality}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space with $\kappa>0$ and let $D=B(o,r)$ be a $\kappa$-domain, for some $o\in \mathcal{X}$ and $r\in [0,\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa))$. Let $x,y,z\in D$ and $\gamma\in \Gamma(x,z)$, $\eta\in\Gamma(y,z)$.
Then, for every $t\in [0,1]$,
\[
\mathrm{d}_o(\gamma(t),\eta(t))\leq (1-\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)t) \mathrm{d}_o(x,y)
\]
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:stability_same_endpoint}
With the notation of Proposition~\ref{prop:endpoint-inequality} above, we have, for all $t\in[0,1]$,
\[
\limsup_{\substack{y\to x \\ y\in D\setminus\{x\}}}
\frac{d(\gamma(t),\eta(t))}{d(x,y)}
\leq
\frac{\sin((1-t)d(x,z)\sqrt\kappa)}{\sin(d(x,z)\sqrt\kappa)}
\]
Here $\eta\in\Gamma(y,z)$ is the unique geodesic corresponding to each $y$, and the right-hand side is understood as $1-t$ if $x=z$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. The cases $t=0$ and $t=1$ are immediate, so fix $t\in(0,1)$.
Let $\xi:[0,1]\to B(o,r)$ be an absolutely continuous path joining $x$ and $y$. For each $s\in[0,1]$, let $\lambda_s\in\Gamma(\xi(s),z)$ and set $\xi_t(s):=\lambda_s(t)$: This defines a new path $\xi_t:[0,1]\to\mathcal{X}$. Since $B(o,r)$ is geodesically convex, $\xi_t(s)\in B(o,r)$ for all $s\in[0,1]$. For an illustration of this construction, see Figure~\ref{fig:common-endpoint-stability}.

If, for $p,q\in D$, we denote by $\gamma_{p,q}$ the unique geodesic from $p$ to $q$, then Lemma~\ref{lem:stability_same_endpoint} gives an infinitesimal bound for the radial map $p\in D\mapsto\gamma_{p,z}(t)$, uniformly bounded above by $2r(1-t)/\sin(2r)$. By the mean value inequality along geodesics in $D$, this map is Lipschitz. Its composition with $\xi$ is therefore absolutely continuous, showing that $\xi_t\in A(\gamma(t),\eta(t))$.

Therefore, by \cite[Theorem~1.1.2]{ambrosio2005gradient}, both ratios $\frac{\mathrm d(\xi(s'),\xi(s))} {|s'-s|}$ and $\frac{\mathrm d(\xi_t(s'),\xi_t(s))} {|s'-s|}$ have limits as $s'\to s$ for almost all $s\in [0,1]$, with respect to the Lebesgue measure -- these limits are $|\Dot\xi|(s)$ and $|\Dot\xi_t|(s)$ respectively, as per Definition~\ref{def:metric-speed}. Let $s\in [0,1]$ be such a number for which both limits exist. If $|\Dot\xi|(s)\neq 0$, then Lemma~\ref{lem:stability_same_endpoint} yields that 
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \frac{|\Dot\xi_t|(s)}{|\Dot\xi|(s)} = \lim_{\substack{s'\to s \\ s'\ne s}} \frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi_t(s'),\xi_t(s))}{\mathrm{d}(\xi(s'),\xi(s))} \leq \frac{\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(\xi(s),z))}{\sin\mathrm{d}(\xi(s),z)}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
where the right-hand side is understood as $1-t$ if $\xi(s)=z$. If $|\Dot\xi|(s)=0$, let $(s_n)_{n\geq 1}$ be any sequence of numbers in $[0,1]\setminus\{s\}$ with $s_n\to s$ as $n\to\infty$. If $\xi(s_n)=\xi(s)$ for all large enough $n$, then it also holds that $\xi_t(s_n)=\xi_t(s)$ for all large enough $n$, so
$$|\Dot\xi_t|(s)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi_t(s_n),\xi_t(s))}{|s_n-s|}=0=|\Dot\xi|(s).$$
Otherwise, consider a subsequence $(s_{k_n})_{n\geq 1}$, with $k_n\to\infty$ as $n\to\infty$, such that $\xi(s_{k_n})\neq\xi(s)$ for all $n\geq 1$. Then, again by Lemma~\ref{lem:stability_same_endpoint},
$$\infty>\limsup_{n\to\infty} \frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi_t(s_{k_n}),\xi_t(s))}{\mathrm{d}(\xi(s_{k_n}),\xi(s))}=\limsup_{n\to\infty} \frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi_t(s_{k_n}),\xi_t(s))}{|s_{k_n}-s|} \left(\frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi(s_{k_n}),\xi(s))}{|s_{k_n}-s|}\right)^{-1}$$
so necessarily, 
$$|\Dot\xi_t|(s)=\lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\mathrm{d}(\xi_t(s_{k_n}),\xi_t(s))}{|s_{k_n}-s|}=0.$$
In every case, we have established that $$|\Dot\xi_t|(s)\leq |\Dot\xi|(s)\frac{\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(\xi(s),z))}{\sin\mathrm{d}(\xi(s),z)}.$$


Dividing by $\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))>0$, we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eqn:proof_31415}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\frac{|\Dot\xi_t|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}
&\leq
\frac{|\Dot\xi|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))}
\frac{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))\sin((1-t)\mathrm d(\xi(s),z))}
     {\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))\sin\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

Our goal now is to show that the second ratio on the right-hand side is bounded by $1-t\cos r$,
independently of $s$.

Suppose first that $z\neq o$ and $\xi(s)\neq z$. By the triangle inequality,
\[
\mathrm d(z,\xi(s))+\mathrm d(z,o)+\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))
\leq4r<2\pi.
\]
Hence, by
\cite[Proposition~II.1.7(3)]{bridsonhaefliger1999},
\[
\angle_z^{(1)}(o,\xi_t(s))
\leq\angle_z^{(1)}(o,\xi(s)).
\]
Since $\cos$ is decreasing on $[0,\pi]$,
\[
\cos\angle_z^{(1)}(o,\xi_t(s))
\geq\cos\angle_z^{(1)}(o,\xi(s)).
\]
Using Definition~\ref{def:comparison_angles}, we obtain
\[
\frac{
\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))
-\cos\mathrm d(o,z)\cos\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)}
{\sin\mathrm d(o,z)\sin\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)}\geq
\frac{
\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))
-\cos\mathrm d(o,z)\cos\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}
{\sin\mathrm d(o,z)\sin\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}.
\]

Multiplying by the positive denominator on the left gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
&\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))
-\cos\mathrm d(o,z)\cos\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)\\
&\qquad\geq
\left(
\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))
-\cos\mathrm d(o,z)\cos\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)
\right)
\frac{\sin\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)}
     {\sin\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Note that if $z=o$ (but still, $\xi(s)\neq z$), this last inequality holds trivially, and it is in fact an equality. Rearranging and setting $a:=\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)\in(0,\pi)$, we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
&\frac{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))\sin((1-t)\mathrm d(\xi(s),z))}
     {\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))\sin\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}\\
&\quad\leq
1-\frac{\cos\mathrm d(o,z)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}
\left(
\cos\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)
-\frac{\cos\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)\sin\mathrm d(\xi_t(s),z)}
      {\sin\mathrm d(\xi(s),z)}
\right)\\
&\quad=
1-\frac{\cos\mathrm d(o,z)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}
\frac{\cos((1-t)a)\sin a-\cos a\sin((1-t)a)}
     {\sin a}\\
&\quad=
1-\frac{\cos\mathrm d(o,z)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}
\frac{\sin(ta)}{\sin a}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

The desired bound follows since $\cos\mathrm d(o,z)\geq\cos r$ and $\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))\leq1$. Moreover, concavity of $\sin$ on $[0,\pi]$ gives $\sin(ta)/\sin a\geq t$.

Now, when $\xi(s)=z$, the second ratio on the right-hand side of \eqref{eqn:proof_31415} is understood as $1-t$, which is also at most $1-t\cos r$.

Finally, we have established that for almost every $s\in[0,1]$,  
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\frac{|\Dot\xi_t|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}
\leq
(1-t\cos r)
\frac{|\Dot\xi|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Integrating over $s\in[0,1]$ and recalling that $\xi_t\in A(\gamma(t),\eta(t))$, we obtain,
by Definition~\ref{def:weighted-distance},
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathrm d_o(\gamma(t),\eta(t))
&\leq
\int_0^1
\frac{|\Dot\xi_t|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi_t(s))}\,\mathrm d s\\
&\leq
(1-t\cos r)\int_0^1
\frac{|\Dot\xi|(s)}{\cos\mathrm d(o,\xi(s))}\,\mathrm d s.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Taking the infimum over all $\xi\in A(x,y)$ with values in $B(o,r)$ yields the desired result.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:dir-derivative}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain of $\mathcal{X}$, and let $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$ be concentrated on $D$.
For $x,z\in D$, let $\gamma_{xz}\in\Gamma(x,z)$, which is unique by Proposition~\ref{prop:unique-geodesics-domain}.
We define
\[
DF_\mu(x)[\log_x z]
:=
\lim_{t\downarrow0}
\frac{F_\mu(\gamma_{xz}(t))-F_\mu(x)}{t}.
\]
Then this quantity exists and is given by
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:dir-derivative}
DF_\mu(x)[\log_x z]
=
-\int_D
\big\langle\log_x y,\log_x z\big\rangle_x
\,\mathrm d\mu(y).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Moreover, defining the directional derivative along all
geodesics issuing from $x$, with arbitrary nonnegative speeds,
yields a function on $\widetilde C_x\mathcal{X}$ that extends uniquely
to a Lipschitz function on $C_x\mathcal{X}$, still denoted
\[
DF_\mu(x)[v]
=-\int_D\langle\log_x y,v\rangle_x\,\mathrm d\mu(y),
\qquad v\in C_x\mathcal{X}.
\]
Its Lipschitz constant is at most
$\int_D\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm d\mu(y)<\infty$.
In particular, if $D=B(o,r)$ with $r<\infty$,
this extension is $2r$-Lipschitz.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:cosine-frechet-transfer}
For every $x,z\in D$,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:integrated-transfer-upper}
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(z)
\leq
-\frac1{\kappa}\int_D
\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,y)}
     {\sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,y)\bigr)}
\Bigl[
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,y)\bigr)
-
\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(z,y)\bigr)
\Bigr]
\,\mathrm d\mu(y).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
The quotient $u/\sin u$ is understood as $1$ at $u=0$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. Consider
\[
    H\colon(-1,1]\longrightarrow[0,\pi^2),
    \qquad
    H(u):=\arccos^2(u).
\]
If \(u=\cos\theta\), with \(\theta\in(0,\pi)\), then
\[
    H'(u)=-\frac{2\theta}{\sin\theta},
    \qquad
    H''(u)
    =
    2\frac{\sin\theta-\theta\cos\theta}{\sin^3\theta}\geq0.
\]
Thus \(H\) is convex. 
Fix $y\in D$. Since $\mathrm d(x,y),\mathrm d(z,y)\leq2r<\pi$, the first-order convexity inequality for $H$ gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
H(\cos\mathrm d(z,y))-H(\cos\mathrm d(x,y))
&\geq H'(\cos\mathrm d(x,y))\bigl(\cos\mathrm d(z,y)-\cos\mathrm d(x,y)\bigr).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Using $H(\cos u)=u^2$ for $u\in[0,\pi)$, we obtain
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathrm d^2(x,y)-\mathrm d^2(z,y)
&\leq
-2\frac{\mathrm d(x,y)}{\sin\mathrm d(x,y)}
\bigl(\cos\mathrm d(x,y)-\cos\mathrm d(z,y)\bigr).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Integration with respect to \(\mu\), followed by division by \(2\), proves
\eqref{eq:integrated-transfer-upper}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:directional-derivative-transfer}
For every $x,z\in D$,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:directional-transfer-hemispherical}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(z)
&\leq
-\frac{\sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr)}
      {\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,z)}
    DF_\mu(x)[\log_x z]\\
&\quad
-\frac{1-\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr)}
      {\kappa\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,o)\bigr)}
    \left[
        \cos(r\sqrt\kappa)
        +
        \kappa
        \frac{\sin\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,o)\bigr)}
             {\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d(x,o)}
        DF_\mu(x)[\log_x o]
    \right].
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
The quotient $\sin u/u$ is understood as $1$ at $u=0$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. The case $x=z$ is immediate, so assume $x\neq z$.
For every $y\in D\setminus\{x\}$, all distances between
$x,z,y$ are strictly smaller than $\pi$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:cosine-comparison-domain},
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:extended-angle-comparison-transfer}
\frac{
    \cos\mathrm d(z,y)-\cos\mathrm d(x,z)\cos\mathrm d(x,y)
}{
    \sin\mathrm d(x,z)\sin\mathrm d(x,y)
}
\leq
\cos\angle_x(z,y).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Multiplying \eqref{eq:extended-angle-comparison-transfer} by
$\mathrm d(x,y)\sin\mathrm d(x,z)$, we obtain
\[
\frac{\mathrm d(x,y)}{\sin\mathrm d(x,y)}
\Bigl(
    \cos\mathrm d(z,y)
    -
    \cos\mathrm d(x,z)\cos\mathrm d(x,y)
\Bigr)
\leq
\frac{\sin\mathrm d(x,z)}{\mathrm d(x,z)}
\left\langle\log_x z,\log_x y\right\rangle_x.
\]
This inequality also holds when $y=x$, with both sides equal to zero, using the continuous extension of $u/\sin u$.

Together with
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\cos\mathrm d(z,y)-\cos\mathrm d(x,y)
&=
\Bigl(\cos\mathrm d(z,y)-\cos\mathrm d(x,z)\cos\mathrm d(x,y)\Bigr)\\
&\quad-
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr)\cos\mathrm d(x,y),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
and recalling from Proposition~\ref{prop:dir-derivative} the directional derivative of $F_\mu$, Lemma~\ref{lem:cosine-frechet-transfer} gives
\[
    F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(z)
    \leq
-\frac{\sin d(x,z)}{d(x,z)}
    D F_\mu(x)[\log_xz]
    -
    \bigl(1-\cos d(x,z)\bigr)J_\mu(x),
\]
where
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:def-Jmu}
J_\mu(x):=
\int_\mathcal{X}
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\cot\bigl(\mathrm{d}(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Here $u\cot u$ is understood as $1$ at $u=0$.

We need to lower bound $J_\mu(x)$. Naively, one could use the boundedness of the support:
since $x,y\in D=B(o,r)$, we have $\mathrm d(x,y)\leq2r<\pi$, and the monotonicity of $u\mapsto u\cot u$ gives $J_\mu(x)\geq2r\cot(2r)$. However, this lower bound is nonpositive when $r\geq\pi/4$. We therefore need a bound better suited to our setting. To address this issue, which will also arise later, the idea is to relate $x$ and $y$ in the integrand to
the centre $o$ of the ball. This is similar to the approach in \cite{afsari2011}.

If $x=o$, then $J_\mu(o)\geq\cos r$ and
$DF_\mu(o)[\log_o o]=0$, so the preceding bound proves
the claim. We may therefore assume $\mathrm d(x,o)>0$.

By Proposition~\ref{prop:cosine-comparison-domain},
\[
\frac{\mathrm d(x,y)}{\sin\mathrm d(x,y)}
\Bigl[
\cos\mathrm d(o,y)-\cos\mathrm d(x,o)\cos\mathrm d(x,y)
\Bigr]
\leq
\frac{\sin\mathrm d(x,o)}{\mathrm d(x,o)}
\langle\log_x o,\log_x y\rangle_x.
\]
The inequality also holds when $y=x$, by continuous extension. Integrating and using Proposition~\ref{prop:dir-derivative}, we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:afsari-inequality}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\cos\mathrm d(x,o)\,J_\mu(x)
&\geq
\int_D\frac{\mathrm d(x,y)}{\sin\mathrm d(x,y)}
\cos\mathrm d(o,y)\,\mathrm d\mu(y)\\
&\quad+
\frac{\sin\mathrm d(x,o)}{\mathrm d(x,o)}
DF_\mu(x)[\log_x o].
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Since $\mathrm d(x,y)/\sin\mathrm d(x,y)\geq1$ and $\cos\mathrm d(o,y)\geq\cos r>0$, substitution into the
preceding bound proves the desired inequality.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:existence_uniqueness}
The Fréchet function $F_\mu$ admits a unique minimizer $b(\mu)$ over $\mathcal{X}$, and $b(\mu)\in D$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:bary_in_convex_hull}
Let $C\subseteq D$ be a closed geodesically convex set such that $\mu(C)=1$. Denote by $b:=b(\mu)\in D$ the unique barycenter of $\mu$. Then $b\in C$.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. For brevity, write $b:=b(\mu)$.
Since $\mu(C)=1$,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    2F_\mu(b) = \int_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}^2(b,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x) = \int_{C}\mathrm{d}^2(b,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x) \geq \mathrm{d}^2(b,C)\mu(C) = \mathrm{d}^2(b,C),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
so 
$$\mathrm d(b,C)^2\leq 2F_\mu(b)\leq 2F_\mu(o)\leq r^2,$$
hence $\mathrm d(b,C)\leq r<\pi/2$. By contradiction, assume that $b\in B(o,r)\setminus C$.
By Proposition~\ref{lem:projection},
we can consider its projection onto $C$, denoted by
$p:=p_C(b)$.
Since $C\subseteq B(o,r)$, all distances between $b,p,y$,
for $y\in C$, are less than $\pi$.

Let $y\in C\setminus\{p\}$. Using Proposition~\ref{prop:cosine-comparison-domain} and the obtuse angle property \eqref{eqn:obtuse} from Proposition~\ref{lem:projection},
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:projection-inequality}
\cos\mathrm d(b,y)\leq\cos\mathrm d(p,b)\cos\mathrm d(p,y).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
This inequality also holds when $y=p$, with equality.

Unlike in \cite[Proposition~3]{gietl2024}, we cannot directly conclude that $\mathrm d(p,y)\leq\mathrm d(b,y)$ under our weaker support assumption.
Nevertheless, we can study the directional derivative of $F_\mu$ at $b$ towards $p$ (see~\eqref{eq:dir-derivative}). Using \eqref{eq:dir-derivative} and again
Proposition~\ref{prop:cosine-comparison-domain}, now applied at $b$ to $(b,p,y)$ (where $b\notin\{p,y\}$ by assumption), we obtain, since $\mu(C)=1$,
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
-DF_\mu(b)[\log_b p]
&=\int_C\langle\log_b p,\log_b y\rangle_b\,\mathrm d\mu(y)\\
&\geq\frac{\mathrm d(b,p)}{\sin\mathrm d(b,p)}
  \int_C\frac{\mathrm d(b,y)}{\sin\mathrm d(b,y)}
  \left(\cos\mathrm d(p,y)-\cos\mathrm d(b,y)\cos\mathrm d(b,p)\right)\mathrm d\mu(y)\\
&\geq\frac{\mathrm d(b,p)\sin\mathrm d(b,p)}{\cos\mathrm d(b,p)}J_\mu(b).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Here $J_\mu$ is defined in \eqref{eq:def-Jmu}, and the last inequality follows from \eqref{eq:projection-inequality} and $\cos\mathrm d(b,p)>0$. At the barycenter, \eqref{eq:afsari-inequality} and first-order optimality give $J_\mu(b)\geq\cos r>0$. Since $0<\mathrm d(b,p)<\pi/2$, we obtain
$DF_\mu(b)[\log_b p]<0$, contradicting the first-order optimality condition at $b$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k}
Denote by $b:=b(\mu)\in D$ the unique barycenter of $\mu$.
For every $x\in D$,
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)
\geq
\frac{\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)}{\kappa}
\Bigl(1-\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\kappa}\,d(x,b)\bigr)\Bigr).
\]
Consequently, for every $x\in D$,
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)
\geq c_{\mathrm{var}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\,d^2(x,b),
\]
where for all $u\in[0,\pi/2)$
\[
c_{\mathrm{var}}(u):=
\cos(u)\,
\frac{1-\cos(2u)}{4u^2}
>0
\]
and $c_{\mathrm{var}}(0)$ is understood as $1/2$.
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
For fixed $r>0$, $c_{\mathrm{var}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\to\frac12$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$, consistently with the variance inequality in \cite[Proposition~4.4]{sturm2003}, which requires no bounded-support assumption.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:directional-derivative-transfer} with first point \(b\) and second point \(x\). Since \(b\) minimizes \(F_\mu\), the
first-order condition gives
\[
    D F_\mu(b)[\log_{b}x]\geq0,
    \qquad
    D F_\mu(b)[\log_{b}o]\geq0.
\]
Consequently,
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)\geq
\frac{
    1-\cos d(x,b)
}{
    \cos d(b,o)
}
\cos r \geq
\cos r\bigl(1-\cos d(x,b)\bigr).
\]
Since \(d(x,b)\leq2r<\pi\) and the function
\[
    t\longmapsto\frac{1-\cos t}{t^2}
\]
is decreasing on \((0,\pi)\), it follows that
\[
    F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)
    \geq
    \cos r
    \frac{1-\cos(2r)}{4r^2}
    d^2(x,b).
\]
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:wasserstein-one-stability}
Let $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal P(\mathcal{X})$ be supported on $D$, and denote by $b(\mu),b(\nu)\in D$ their respective barycenters. Then
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:wasserstein-one-stability}
\mathrm d(b(\mu),b(\nu))
\leq c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)\,W_1(\mu,\nu),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
where, for every $u\in[0,\pi/2)$, $c_{W_1}(u):=\frac{c_{4\bullet}(u)}{4c_{\mathrm{var}}(u)}$. Here $c_{4\bullet}(u)$ and $c_{\mathrm{var}}(u)$
are the constants in Propositions~\ref{prop:local-four-point} and~\ref{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k}, respectively.
Thus, the barycenter map on probability measures supported on $D$ is $c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)$-Lipschitz with respect to $W_1$ and $\mathrm d$.
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
For fixed $r>0$, $c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)\to1$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$, consistently with \cite[Theorem~6.3]{sturm2003}, which requires no bounded-support assumption.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. Fix a coupling $\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)$. Since both marginals assign
mass one to $B(o,r)$, the coupling assigns mass one to
$B(o,r)\times B(o,r)$. Set
\[
G(y):=d^2(b(\nu),y)-d^2(b(\mu),y).
\]
Applying the quantitative four-point inequality
Proposition~\ref{prop:local-four-point} in the domain
$D=B(o,r)$ of radius $r$ gives
\[
|G(y)-G(z)|
\leq
c_{4\bullet}(r)\,d(b(\mu),b(\nu))d(y,z)
\]
for $\pi$-almost every $(y,z)$. Consequently,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
&F_\mu(b(\nu))-F_\mu(b(\mu))
 +F_\nu(b(\mu))-F_\nu(b(\nu)) \notag\\
&\qquad=
\frac12\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}
\bigl(G(y)-G(z)\bigr)\,\mathrm d\pi(y,z) \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
\frac{c_{4\bullet}(r)}2\,
d(b(\mu),b(\nu))
\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}d(y,z)\,\mathrm d\pi(y,z).
\label{eq:coupling-stability-bound}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
On the other hand, applying
Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k} to $F_\mu$ at $b(\nu)$,
and to $F_\nu$ at $b(\mu)$, yields
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
F_\mu(b(\nu))-F_\mu(b(\mu))
+F_\nu(b(\mu))-F_\nu(b(\nu))
\geq
2c_{\mathrm{var}}(r)\,d^2(b(\mu),b(\nu)).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Combining this bound with \eqref{eq:coupling-stability-bound} and taking the infimum
over $\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ gives
$$22c_{\mathrm{var}}(r)\mathrm{d}^2(b(\mu),b(\nu))\leq \frac{c_{4\bullet}(r)}{2}\mathrm{d}(b(\mu),b(\nu))W_1(\mu,\nu),$$
which proves \eqref{eq:wasserstein-one-stability}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
Following \cite[Definition 3.1]{ohtapalfia2015}, we denote by $|\nabla^-F_\mu|(x)$ the descending metric slope, also called the descending absolute gradient, defined by
\[
|\nabla^-F_\mu|(x)
:=
\max\left\{
0,\,
\limsup_{\substack{y\to x\\y\ne x}}
\frac{F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(y)}{d(x,y)}
\right\}.
\]
This is a nonnegative scalar quantity.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:pl-inequality}
Denote by $b:=b(\mu)\in D$ its unique barycenter.
Then, for every $x\in D$,
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b(\mu))
\leq c_{\mathrm{PL}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\,
|\nabla^-F_\mu|(x)^2,
\]
where one may take for all $u\in[0,\pi/2)$
\[
c_{\mathrm{PL}}(u)
:=
\frac{1}{2\cos^2(u)}.
\]
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
For every fixed $r>0$, $c_{\mathrm{PL}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\to1/2$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$. This is the optimal constant in $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ spaces, where the inequality follows from strong geodesic convexity of the squared distance \cite[Proposition~2.3, inequality~(2.3)]{sturm2003}, without bounded-support assumption, for every $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$.
Fix $x\in B(o,r)$ and set 
\[
h:=d(x,b),\qquad q:=d(x,o),\qquad
s:=|\nabla^-F_\mu|(x).
\]

From Theorem~\ref{thm:directional-derivative-transfer},
by directly applying~\eqref{eq:slope-directional-bound},
we obtain
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)
&\leq
\bigl[\sin h+(1-\cos h)\tan q\bigr]s
-\frac{\cos r}{\cos q}(1-\cos h)\\
&=
\frac{2}{\cos q}
\left[
s\sin(h/2)\cos(h/2-q)
-\cos r\,\sin^2(h/2)
\right]\\
&\leq
\frac{2}{\cos q}
\left[
s\sin(h/2)-\cos r\,\sin^2(h/2)
\right]\\
&=:P\bigl(\sin(h/2)\bigr),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
where $P(u)=(2/\cos q)(su-u^2\cos(r))$ for all $u\in\mathbb R$.
Since $\cos r>0$ and $\cos q\geq\cos r$, we have $P(u)\leq\frac{s^2}{2\cos r\cos q}\leq\frac{s^2}{2\cos^2r}$ for all $u\in\mathbb R$, yielding the result.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:dual-norm-tangent-cone}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $b\in\mathcal{X}$. Let $\nu\in\mathcal P_2(C_b\mathcal{X})$ and denote by $\beta$ its barycenter.
Then
\[
\sup_{\substack{u\in C_b\mathcal{X}\\ |u|_b\leq1}}
\int_{C_b\mathcal{X}}\langle u,v\rangle_b\,\mathrm{d}\nu(v)
=|\beta|_b.
\]
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
In a Hilbert space $H$, the barycenter is the mean $\beta=\int_H v\,\mathrm{d}\nu(v)$, and the preceding identity reduces to the usual dual characterization of the norm
$\|\beta\| =\sup_{\|u\|\leq1}\langle u,\beta\rangle $.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{prop:barycenter-tangent-process}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and let $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$ be concentrated on a $\kappa$-domain $D$ of $\mathcal{X}$. Let $b=b(\mu)$ be the unique barycenter of $\mu$.

Then, for every $x\in D$,
\[
\mathrm{d}(x,b)\leq c_{\rm tan}(r,\kappa)
\sup_{\substack{u\in C_x\mathcal{X}\\ |u|_x\leq1}}
\int_D\langle\log_x y,u\rangle_x\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y),
\]
where
\[
c_{\rm tan}(r,\kappa):=
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    {\begin{dcases}}{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
1 & \mbox{if }\kappa\leq0,\\
\dfrac{r\sqrt{\kappa}}
{\sin(r\sqrt{\kappa})\cos^2(r\sqrt{\kappa})}
& \mbox{if }\kappa>0,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@dcases} 
    {\end{dcases}}{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@dcases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
and $r\in (0,\infty]$ is the radius of $D$.\footnote{Recall that $r$ can be infinite if $\kappa\leq 0$ and that $r<\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$ if $\kappa>0$.}
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\kappa>0$. We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}$. In that case, note that the radius of $D$ satisfies $r<\pi/2$.
Set
\[
h:=\mathrm{d}(x,b),\qquad
S:=\sup_{\substack{u\in C_x\mathcal{X}\\ |u|_x\leq1}}
\int_D\langle\log_x y,u\rangle_x\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y).
\]
The case $h=0$ is immediate. As in
\eqref{eq:slope-directional-bound}, the directional derivative formula and the definition of $S$ give $DF_\mu(x)[\log_x z]\geq-\mathrm{d}(x,z)S$ for every $z\in D$.
We then follow the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:pl-inequality}, with $S$ in place of the descending slope. Theorem~\ref{thm:directional-derivative-transfer} gives
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)
\leq
\bigl[\sin h+(1-\cos h)\tan r\bigr]S
-\cos r(1-\cos h).
\]
By Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k},
\[
F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)\geq\cos r(1-\cos h).
\]
Combining these inequalities and using the half-angle
identities, we obtain
\[
S\geq
\frac{2\cos r(1-\cos h)}
{\sin h+(1-\cos h)\tan r}
=
\frac{2\cos^2r\,\sin(h/2)}{\cos(r-h/2)}
\geq2\cos^2r\,\sin(h/2)
\geq\cos^2r\,\frac{\sin r}{r}\,h.
\]
where the last inequality follows from $h/2\leq r$ and the monotonicity of $\sin u/u$ on $(0,\pi)$. This proves the positive-curvature case.

Now, assume $\kappa\leq0$, and define $h$ and $S$ as above using the original metric $\mathrm{d}$. The case $h=0$ is immediate. Otherwise, observe that $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space,
so the Fr\'echet function is $1$-strongly geodesically convex by \cite[Proposition~2.3, inequality~(2.3)]{sturm2003}.

We give the details
here for the reader's convenience. Let $\gamma\in\Gamma(x,b)$ and note that strong geodesic convexity yields, for $t\in(0,1)$,
\[
F_\mu(\gamma(t)) \leq(1-t)F_\mu(x)+tF_\mu(b)
-\frac12t(1-t)h^2.
\]
Subtracting $F_\mu(x)$, dividing by $t$, and letting
$t\downarrow0$, we obtain
\[
DF_\mu(x)[\log_x b]
\leq F_\mu(b)-F_\mu(x)-\frac12h^2
\leq-h^2,
\]
where the last inequality is the variance inequality in $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ spaces \cite[Proposition 4.4]{sturm2003}. Since $DF_\mu(x)[\log_x b]\geq-hS$, this yields $h\leq S$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{th:empirical-expected-error}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be i.i.d.\ with law $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$, where $n\geq 1$ is an integer.
If $\kappa>0$, assume additionally that $\mu$ is concentrated
on a $\kappa$-domain of radius $r>0$.
Let $b:=b(\mu)$ and $\widehat b_n:=b(\mu_n)$, and set
$\sigma_\mu^2:=\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(X_1,b)]$. Then
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(\widehat b_n,b)]
\leq c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa)\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n}
\]
where $c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa):=c_{\mathrm{tan}}(r,\kappa)^2$, with $c_{\mathrm{tan}}$ defined in
Proposition~\ref{prop:barycenter-tangent-process}.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
Recall that if $\kappa\leq 0$, then $c_{\textrm{tan}}(r,\kappa)=1$. Hence, in the $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ case, the theorem improves the constant $2$ in \cite[Theorem~3]{brunelserres2025} to the optimal Hilbert-space constant $1$, addressing the nonpositive-curvature case of their Open Question~2.
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   
Finally, we emphasize that
our tangent-cone argument, inspired by \cite{bartl2025}, requires no additional lower curvature bound, unlike \cite[Theorem~1.3]{legouic2022}.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\kappa>0$. We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}$.
Set $V_i:=\log_bX_i$, and let $\beta_n$ be the barycenter in $C_b\mathcal{X}$ of $n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^n\delta_{V_i}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:barycenter-tangent-process} applied to $\mu_n$ at $b$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:dual-norm-tangent-cone},
\[
\mathrm{d}(\widehat b_n,b)
\leq c_{\mathrm{tan}}(r,1)|\beta_n|_b.
\]
It therefore remains to show that $\mathbb E[|\beta_n|_b^2]\leq\sigma_\mu^2/n$.
Set $A_n(u):=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^n\langle u,V_i\rangle_b$.
The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:dual-norm-tangent-cone} also gives, for every $u\in C_b\mathcal{X}$,
\[
A_n(u)\leq\langle u,\beta_n\rangle_b.
\]
Let $m_n$ be the point at time $1/(n+1)$ on the geodesic from $\beta_n$ to $V_{n+1}$. Then
\[
A_{n+1}(u)
\leq
\frac n{n+1}\langle u,\beta_n\rangle_b
+\frac1{n+1}\langle u,V_{n+1}\rangle_b.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:dual-norm-tangent-cone}, applied to the empirical measure and to $\frac n{n+1}\delta_{\beta_n}+\frac1{n+1}\delta_{V_{n+1}}$,
\[
|\beta_{n+1}|_b
=\sup_{|u|_b\leq1}A_{n+1}(u)
\leq|m_n|_b.
\]
The $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ inequality in $C_b\mathcal{X}$ therefore gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
|\beta_{n+1}|_b^2
&\leq
\frac n{n+1}|\beta_n|_b^2
+\frac1{n+1}|V_{n+1}|_b^2
-\frac n{(n+1)^2}
\mathrm{d}_{C_b\mathcal{X}}^2(\beta_n,V_{n+1})\\
&=
\frac{n^2|\beta_n|_b^2+|V_{n+1}|_b^2
+2n\langle\beta_n,V_{n+1}\rangle_b}{(n+1)^2}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
The dual norm identity and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
|\beta_n|_b^2\leq\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n|V_i|_b^2,
\]
so $\mathbb E[|\beta_n|_b^2]\leq\sigma_\mu^2$. Consequently, $\langle\beta_n,V_{n+1}\rangle_b$
is integrable. First-order optimality at $b$ gives $\mathbb E[\langle u,V_1\rangle_b]\leq0$ for every $u\in C_b\mathcal{X}$. Since $\beta_n$ is measurable with respect to
$\mathcal F_n:=\sigma(X_1,\ldots,X_n)$ (by continuity of $\log_b$, see~\cite[Lemma~1]{brunelserres2025}, and the barycenter contraction property \cite[Theorem~6.3]{sturm2003}), independence yields
\[
\mathbb E[\langle\beta_n,V_{n+1}\rangle_b\mid\mathcal F_n]\leq0.
\]
Taking expectations, we obtain
\[
(n+1)^2\mathbb E[|\beta_{n+1}|_b^2]
\leq n^2\mathbb E[|\beta_n|_b^2]+\sigma_\mu^2.
\]
Since $\beta_1=V_1$ and $\mathbb E[|V_1|_b^2]=\sigma_\mu^2$, iteration gives
\[
\mathbb E[|\beta_n|_b^2]\leq\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n}.
\]
Combining this bound with
$\mathrm{d}(\widehat b_n,b)\leq c_{\mathrm{tan}}(r,1)|\beta_n|_b$
proves the claim.

If $\kappa\leq 0$, the same argument applies without rescaling, with $c_{\mathrm{tan}}(r,\kappa)=1$, and gives \[
\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(\widehat b_n,b)]\leq\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n}.
\]
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:empirical-high-probability}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be i.i.d. with law $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$, where $n\geq 1$ is an integer. Assume that $\mu$ is concentrated on a $\kappa$-domain of radius $r>0$. Let $b:=b(\mu)$ and $\widehat b_n:=b(\mu_n)$, and set $\sigma_\mu^2:=\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(X_1,b)]$. Then, for every $\delta\in(0,1)$, with probability at least
$1-\delta$,
\[
\mathrm d(\widehat b_n,b)
\leq
\sqrt{c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa)}
\sqrt{\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n}}
+
2r\,c_{\mathrm{emp,HP}}(r,\kappa)
\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}},
\]
where 
$$c_{\mathrm{emp,HP}}(r,\kappa)= 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\begin{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
c_{W_1}(r\sqrt{\kappa}) & \text{ if } \kappa>0 \\
1 & \text{ if } \kappa\leq 0
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\end{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  $$
and $c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa)$ and $c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)$
are the constants appearing in Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error} and Proposition~\ref{prop:wasserstein-one-stability}, respectively.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
When $\kappa\leq 0$, the same argument used in \cite[Corollary~1]{brunelserres2025} yields the desired result, with a better constant in front of the expected-error term thanks to Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error}. 
We also follow the sub-Gaussian concentration argument used in \cite[Corollary~1]{brunelserres2025} when $\kappa>0$.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:wasserstein-one-stability} and using the coupling that pairs corresponding observations,
\[
W_1\left(
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\delta_{x_i},
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\delta_{y_i}
\right)
\leq \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\mathrm d(x_i,y_i).
\]
Hence, the map
\[
(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\longmapsto\mathrm d(\widehat b_n,b)
\]
is $c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)/n$-Lipschitz for the $\ell^1$-product distance. Since the observations are independent and supported on $B(o,r)$, the bounded
differences inequality implies that with probability at least $1-\delta$,
\[
\mathrm d(\widehat b_n,b)
\leq \mathbb E[\mathrm d(\widehat b_n,b)]
+2r\,c_{W_1}(r\sqrt\kappa)
\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}.
\]
Finally, Cauchy--Schwarz inequality and Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error} give
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d(\widehat b_n,b)]
\leq \sqrt{\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widehat b_n,b)]}
\leq
\sqrt{c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa)}
\sqrt{\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n}},
\]
which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def:iterated-barycenter}
