Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a geodesic space and $n\geq 1$ be an integer. For $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in \mathcal{X}$ and weights $\lambda=(\lambda_2,\ldots,\lambda_n)\in(0,1)^{n-1}$, their iterated barycenter with weights $\lambda$ is $S_n$, defined recursively by
\[
S_1:=x_1,\qquad
S_k:=\gamma_k(\lambda_k),\quad k=2,\ldots,n,
\]
where $\gamma_k\in\Gamma(S_{k-1},x_k)$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:stability of it bar}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space with $\kappa>0$ and $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain. Let $n\geq 1$ be an integer, $x_1,\ldots,x_n,y_1,\ldots,y_n\in D$ and $\lambda=(\lambda_2,\ldots,\lambda_n)$ with 
$$\lambda_k:=\frac{A}{k-1+A}, \quad k=2,\ldots,n, \qquad A:=\frac{1}{\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)}.$$
Let $S_n^x$ be the iterated barycenter of $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ and $S_n^y$ that of $y_1,\ldots,y_n$, both with weights $\lambda$. Then, 
\[
\mathrm d(S_n^x,S_n^y)
\leq
\frac{c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r\sqrt\kappa)}n
\sum_{i=1}^n\mathrm d(x_i,y_i),
\]
where, for every $u\in[0,\pi/2)$, one may take $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(u):=2u/(\cos^3u\,\sin(2u))$, which is understood as $1$ if $u=0$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
For fixed $r>0$, $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\to1$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$, consistently with
\cite[Lemma~3.1]{Funano10} in $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ spaces, which requires no bounded-domain assumption.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. First, the result is trivial for $n=1$, after noting that $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(u)\geq 1$ for all $u\in [0,\pi/2)$. Let us now assume that $n\geq 2$. 

Set $L:=2r/(\cos r\sin(2r))$ in the following derivations.

For $p,q,u,v\in D$, $\gamma_{p,u}\in\Gamma(p,u)$, $\gamma_{p,v}\in\Gamma(p,v)$, $\gamma_{q,v}\in\Gamma(q,v)$ and $t\in[0,1]$, the triangle inequality gives
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_lip12321}
\mathrm d_o(\gamma_{p,u}(t),\gamma_{q,v}(t))\leq
\mathrm d_o(\gamma_{p,v}(t),\gamma_{q,v}(t))+\mathrm d_o(\gamma_{p,u}(t),\gamma_{p,v}(t)).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

The common-endpoint contraction~\eqref{prop:endpoint-inequality} bounds the first term by $(1-t\cos(r))\mathrm d_o(p,q)$, while
Proposition~\ref{eq:L-convexity}, together with \eqref{eq:equivalence-inequality}, bounds the second term by $tL\mathrm d_o(u,v)$. Hence, \eqref{eqn:proof_lip12321} becomes 
$$\mathrm d_o(\gamma_{p,u}(t),\gamma_{q,v}(t)) \leq (1-t\,\cos(r))\mathrm{d}_o(p,q)+ tL\mathrm{d}_o(u,v).$$

Apply this bound to $p=S_{k-1}^x$, $q=S_{k-1}^y$, $u=x_k$, $v=y_k$ and $t=\lambda_k$ for all $k=2,\ldots,n$ yields
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \mathrm{d}_o(S_k^x,S_k^y) \leq \left(1-\frac{1}{k-1+A}\right)\mathrm{d}_o(S_{k-1}^x,S_{k-1}^y)+\frac{AL}{k-1+A}\mathrm{d}_o(x_k,y_k).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Now, multiplying both sides by $k-1+A$ yields
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    (k-1+A)\mathrm{d}_o(S_k^x,S_k^y) \leq (k-2+A)\mathrm{d}_o(S_{k-1}^x,S_{k-1}^y)+AL\mathrm{d}_o(x_k,y_k).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Now, summing those inequalities from $k=2$ to $n$, we obtain
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    (n-1+A)\mathrm{d}_o(S_n^x,S_n^y) & \leq A\mathrm{d}_o(S_1^x,S_1^y)+AL\sum_{k=2}^n \mathrm{d}_o(x_k,y_k) \\
    & = A\mathrm{d}_o(x_1,y_1)+AL\sum_{k=2}^n \mathrm{d}_o(x_k,y_k) \\
    & \leq AL\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{d}_o(x_i,y_i),
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
after noting that $L\geq 1$. Therefore, we obtain that 
$$\mathrm{d}_o(S_n^x,S_n^y) \leq \frac{AL}{n-1+A}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{d}_o(x_i,y_i)\leq \frac{AL}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{d}_o(x_i,y_i)$$
since $n-1+A\geq n$, using that $A=1/\cos(r)\geq 1$. 
We finally obtain the desired result by recalling that $\mathrm d\leq\mathrm d_o\leq\mathrm d/\cos r$ from \eqref{eq:equivalence-inequality} and that $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r)=A^2L$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:iterated-exp}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$ and $D$ be $\kappa$-domain. Let $\mu\in\mathcal P(\mathcal{X})$ with $\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq D$ and let $b=b(\mu)\in D$. Fix some $x\in D$ and let $Y$ be a random variable with law $\mu$. Denote by $\gamma\in\Gamma(x,Y)$ the almost surely unique, random geodesic from $x$ to $Y$. Then, for every $t\in(0,1)$,
\[
\mathbb E\bigl[\Phi_b(\gamma(t))\bigr]
\leq
\bigl(1-2\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)t\bigr)\Phi_b(x)
+
K(r\sqrt\kappa)\,\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(x,Y)]\,t^2.
\]
where
\[
K(u):=\frac{1}{2\cos u}
+\frac{\sin^2u}{\cos^3u}
+\frac{3u\sin u}{\cos^4u},
\qquad 0\leq u<\pi/2.
\]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$. Indeed, rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$ multiplies both $\Phi_b$ and the squared distance by $\kappa$; dividing the normalized inequality by $\kappa$ gives the general case.
Set
\[
c:=\cos r,\qquad \ell:=\mathrm d(x,Y),\qquad
C:=\cos\mathrm d(x,o),\qquad A:=1-\cos\mathrm d(x,b).
\]
Define
\[
\alpha_t:=\frac{\sin((1-t)\ell)}{\sin\ell},
\qquad
\beta_t:=\frac{\sin(t\ell)}{\sin\ell},
\]
with continuous extensions when $\ell=0$.

The cosine inequality along geodesics, Proposition~\ref{prop:geodesic-cosine-inequality}, gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
1-\cos\mathrm d(\gamma(t),b)
&\leq N(t)
:=1-\alpha_t\cos\mathrm d(x,b)-\beta_t\cos\mathrm d(Y,b),\\
\cos\mathrm d(\gamma(t),o)
&\geq H(t)
:=\alpha_t\cos\mathrm d(x,o)+\beta_t\cos\mathrm d(Y,o).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Since $H(t)\geq c>0$ (because $\mathrm{d}(x,o),\mathrm{d}(Y,o)\leq r<\pi/2$) and $N(t)\geq0$, we obtain
\[
\Phi_b(\gamma(t))\leq R(t):=\frac{N(t)}{H(t)}.
\]

For $z\in\{b,o\}$, denote by
\[
D_x(Y,z):=\frac{\ell}{\sin\ell}
\bigl[\cos\mathrm d(Y,z)-\cos\ell\cos\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr],
\]
with continuous extension when $\ell=0$.

Using $\alpha_t=\cos(t\ell)-\beta_t\cos\ell$, we obtain
\[
N(0)=A,\quad N'(0)=-D_x(Y,b),\qquad
H(0)=C,\quad H'(0)=D_x(Y,o).
\]
Consequently,
\[
R(0)=\Phi_b(x),\qquad R'(0)=-G(Y),
\]
where
\[
G(Y):=\frac{D_x(Y,b)}C+\frac{A\,D_x(Y,o)}{C^2}.
\]

We want to apply Taylor's formula to $R$. To do so, we need to control uniformly the remainder term, \emph{i.e.}, the second derivative of $R$. Set $U:=1-N$ and $W:=UH'-U'H$. Since $U''=-\ell^2U$ and $H''=-\ell^2H$, $W$ is constant. Differentiating $R=(1-U)/H$ gives
\[
R''=\frac{\ell^2}{H}
+\frac{2(H')^2}{H^3}
-\frac{2WH'}{H^3}.
\]

The definition of $H$ depends only on the side lengths of $(o,x,Y)$, not on the geodesic itself or the ambient space $\mathcal{X}$. We can therefore directly study $H$
and its derivative on the sphere. The triangle $(o,x,Y)$ has perimeter at most $4r<2\pi$.
Let $(\bar o,\bar x,\bar Y)$ be its spherical comparison triangle, and let $\bar\gamma\in\Gamma(\bar x,\bar Y)$.
Then
\[
H(t)=\cos\rho(t),
\qquad
\rho(t):=\mathrm d_{\mathbb S^2}(\bar\gamma(t),\bar o).
\]

Since the spherical ball $B(\bar o,r)$ is geodesically convex, $\rho(t)\leq r$, and hence $H(t)\geq c$. For $\ell>0$ and $t<1$ such that $\rho(t)>0$, apply Proposition~\ref{prop:first-variation} to the geodesic $u\mapsto\bar\gamma(t+(1-t)u)$.
After dividing by $1-t$, this gives
\[
\rho'_+(t)
=-\ell\cos\angle_{\bar\gamma(t)}(\bar Y,\bar o).
\]
Consequently,
\[
H'(t)
=\ell\sin\rho(t)\cos\angle_{\bar\gamma(t)}(\bar Y,\bar o),
\qquad
|H'(t)|\leq\ell\sin r.
\]
If $\rho(t)=0$ or $\ell=0$, then $H'(t)=0$. The bound at $t=1$ follows by continuity.
Thus,
\[
H(t)\geq c,\qquad |H'(t)|\leq\ell\sin r.
\]

Now, $W$ is constant, so
\[
W=W(0)
=\frac{\ell}{\sin\ell}
\bigl[
\cos\mathrm d(x,b)\cos\mathrm d(Y,o)
-\cos\mathrm d(Y,b)\cos\mathrm d(x,o)
\bigr].
\]
Using the identity $ab-cd=(a-c)b+c(b-d)$, $|\cos(u)|\leq 1$, $\ell\leq2r$ and the monotonicity of $u/\sin u$ on $[0,\pi)$, we obtain
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
|W|
&\leq\frac{\ell}{\sin\ell}
\Bigl(
|\cos\mathrm d(x,b)-\cos\mathrm d(Y,b)|
+|\cos\mathrm d(x,o)-\cos\mathrm d(Y,o)|
\Bigr)\\
&\leq\frac{3\sin r\,\ell^2}{\sin\ell}\leq 3\ell\sin r\,\frac{2r}{\sin(2r)}=\frac{3r\ell}{c}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Consequently,
\[
R''\leq
\frac{\ell^2}{c}
+\frac{2\ell^2\sin^2r}{c^3}
+\frac{6r\ell^2\sin r}{c^4}
=2K(r)\ell^2.
\]
Taylor's formula with integral remainder therefore gives
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eqn:proof_phib}
\Phi_b(\gamma(t))
\leq\Phi_b(x)-tG(Y)
+K(r)\,\mathrm d^2(x,Y)t^2.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

The following estimate uses the integrated cosine-to-Fr\'echet transfer inequality~\eqref{eq:integrated-transfer-upper}, which also underlies Theorem~\ref{thm:directional-derivative-transfer} through angle comparison:
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathbb E[G(Y)]
&\geq
\frac{F_\mu(x)-F_\mu(b)+\Phi_b(x)\cos r}
     {\cos\mathrm d(x,o)}\\
&\geq
\frac{\cos r\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x,b)\bigr)}
     {\cos\mathrm d(x,o)}
+\Phi_b(x)\cos r\\
&=2\cos r\,\Phi_b(x).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Here the first inequality follows by expanding $G(Y)$ and using that $\mathrm d(x,Y)/\sin\mathrm d(x,Y)\geq1$ and that $\cos\mathrm d(o,Y)\geq\cos r$ almost surely. The second follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k} and the fact that $0<\cos\mathrm d(x,o)\leq1$.
Taking expectations in \eqref{eqn:proof_phib} then proves the normalized inequality
\[
\mathbb E[\Phi_b(\gamma(t))]
\leq(1-2\cos r\,t)\Phi_b(x)
+K(r)\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(x,Y)]\,t^2.
\]
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:discrete-recurrence}
Let $(a_n)_{n\geq1}$ be a sequence of nonnegative numbers
satisfying
\[
a_{n+1}
\leq
\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{n+A}\right)a_n
+\frac{\beta}{(n+A)^2}, \qquad \forall n\geq 1,
\]
where $\alpha>1$, $\beta\geq0$, and $A\geq\alpha-1$.
Then, for every $n\geq1$,
\[
a_n\leq\frac{C}{n+A},
\qquad
C:=\max\left\{(1+A)a_1,\frac{\beta}{\alpha-1}\right\}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{th:iterated-expected-error}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$ and $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain. Let $\mu\in\mathcal P(\mathcal{X})$ with $\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq D$ and $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be i.i.d. random variables with law $\mu$, for some integer $n\geq 1$. Let $b=b(\mu), \sigma_\mu^2=\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(X_1,b)]$ and $\widetilde b_n$ be the iterated barycenter of $(X_1,\ldots,X_n)$, with weights
\[
\lambda_k:=\frac{A}{k-1+A},\qquad 2\leq k\leq n,
\]
where we set $A:=1/\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)$. Then, for every $n\geq1$,
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widetilde b_n,b)]
\leq c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})
\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n+A},
\]
where, for every $u\in[0,\pi/2)$, one may take
\[
c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(u):=
\frac{4K(u)(c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(u)^2+1)}{\cos^4u},
\]
with $K$ and $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}$ defined in Lemma~\ref{lem:iterated-exp} and Theorem~\ref{thm:stability of it bar}, respectively.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
For fixed $r>0$, $c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\to4$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$. This is consistent with the variance-over-sample-size rate in \cite[proof of Theorem~4.7]{sturm2003}, which requires
no bounded-support assumption, but does not recover the optimal constant $1$. Obtaining this constant in the zero-curvature limit remains open for our argument, although we expect that a finer analysis could achieve this.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$, which multiplies $\Phi_b$, the squared distances
and $\sigma_\mu^2$ by $\kappa$. Set $V_n:=\Phi_b(\widetilde b_n)$, $A:=1/\cos r$ and $L:=c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r)$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:stability of it bar}, comparing with
the constant sample $(b,\ldots,b)$ and applying the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, we obtain
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widetilde b_n,b)]\leq L^2\sigma_\mu^2.
\]
Consequently, by the triangle inequality,
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widetilde b_n,X_{n+1})]
\leq2(L^2+1)\sigma_\mu^2.
\]

To obtain the upper bound on the expected error, we apply Lemma~\ref{lem:iterated-exp} with $x=\widetilde b_n$,
$Y=X_{n+1}$, and $t=A/(n+A)$. Conditioning on $\mathcal F_n:=\sigma(X_1,\ldots,X_n)$
and then taking expectations gives
\[
\mathbb E[V_{n+1}]
\leq
\left(1-\frac{2}{n+A}\right)\mathbb E[V_n]
+\frac{2K(r)A^2(L^2+1)\sigma_\mu^2}{(n+A)^2}.
\]
Since $A\geq1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:discrete-recurrence}, with $\alpha=2$ and
$\beta=2K(r)A^2(L^2+1)\sigma_\mu^2$, yields
\[
\mathbb E[V_n]\leq
\frac{\max\{(1+A)\mathbb E[V_1],2K(r)A^2(L^2+1)\sigma_\mu^2\}}{n+A}.
\]
Since $\Phi_b(x)\leq\mathrm d^2(x,b)/(2\cos r)$, $A\geq1$, $K(r)\geq1/2$ and $L\geq1$,
\[
(1+A)\mathbb E[V_1]
\leq\frac{A(1+A)}2\sigma_\mu^2
\leq A^2\sigma_\mu^2
\leq2K(r)A^2(L^2+1)\sigma_\mu^2.
\]
Thus,
\[
\mathbb E[V_n]\leq
\frac{2K(r)(L^2+1)}{\cos^2r} \frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n+A}.
\]

Finally, the comparison of $\Phi_b$ with the squared distance and $\sin r\geq r\cos r$ give
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widetilde b_n,b)]\leq\frac{2}{\cos^2r}\mathbb E[V_n]\leq
\frac{4K(r)(L^2+1)}{\cos^4r}
\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n+A}=
c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(r)\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{n+A}.
\]
This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:iterated-exp} gives the following conditional estimate:
$$\mathbb E[\Phi_b(\widetilde b_n)\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]\leq (1-2\cos(r\sqrt\kappa)\lambda_n)\Phi_b(\widetilde b_{n-1})+4r^2K(r\sqrt\kappa)\lambda_n^2,$$
where we used $\mathrm d(\widetilde b_{n-1},X_n)\leq2r$, and $\mathcal F_k:=\sigma(X_1,\ldots,X_k)$ is the $\sigma$-algebra generated by $X_1,\ldots,X_k$, for all $k\geq1$. Since $\sum_n\lambda_n=\infty$ and $\sum_n\lambda_n^2<\infty$, the Robbins--Siegmund theorem~\cite[Theorem~1]{robbinssiegmund1971} implies that $\widetilde b_n\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{} b$ almost surely (for any choice of $A>0$ in the definition of $\widetilde b_n$). This recovers the strong law of large numbers for iterated barycenters proved by Yokota~\cite[Theorem~3]{yokota2018}.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:iterated-high-probability}
With the same notation and assumptions as in Theorem~\ref{th:iterated-expected-error}, for every $\delta\in(0,1)$, with probability at least $1-\delta$,
\[
\mathrm d(\widetilde b_n,b)
\leq
\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\sigma_\mu^2}
           {n+A}}
+
2r\,c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r\sqrt\kappa)
\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}.
\]
Here $c_{\mathrm{E,it}}$ and $c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}$ are the constants in Theorem~\ref{th:iterated-expected-error} and Theorem~\ref{thm:stability of it bar}, respectively.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Since the observations belong to $B(o,r)$, Theorem~\ref{thm:stability of it bar} shows that changing
one observation changes $\mathrm{d}(\widetilde b_n,b)$ by at most $2r\,c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r\sqrt\kappa)/n$.
Arguing as in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{cor:empirical-high-probability}, we obtain,
with probability at least $1-\delta$,
\[
\mathrm d(\widetilde b_n,b)
\leq
\mathbb E[\mathrm d(\widetilde b_n,b)]
+
2r\,c_{\mathrm{it,Lip}}(r\sqrt\kappa)
\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}.
\]
Finally, the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality and
Theorem~\ref{th:iterated-expected-error} give
\[
\mathbb E[\mathrm d(\widetilde b_n,b)]
\leq
\sqrt{\mathbb E[\mathrm d^2(\widetilde b_n,b)]}
\leq
\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathrm{E,it}}(r\sqrt\kappa)\sigma_\mu^2}
           {n+A}},
\]
which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:tightness}
The rate $1/n$ in Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error}
and Theorem~\ref{th:iterated-expected-error}
and their respective corollaries
is optimal, as is immediately seen by specializing to the unit
interval
$(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d})= ([0,1], |\cdot|)$.
Beyond the rate, it is natural to ask if the restriction
$r < D_\kappa/2$ can be relaxed further.
However, as we now show,
any bound $\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(\widehat b_n, b)]
\leq \frac{c_\epsilon}{n}$,
valid uniformly over $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ spaces $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ and measures supported on $B(o, \pi/(2\sqrt\kappa) - \epsilon)$,
must have $c_\epsilon \gtrsim \frac{1}{\epsilon}$.
In particular, no $1/n$ bound with a finite constant can hold uniformly over this class when $r=D_\kappa/2$.

To see this, consider $(\mathcal{X}, \mathrm{d}) = (\mathbb{S}^2, \mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2})$, let $a \in [0,1]$, and let $\mu_a$ be the distribution supported on $\mathbb{S}^2$
defined by
$$
\mu_a := \frac12 (1 - a)(\delta_{x} + \delta_y) + a \delta_z.
$$ for $x = (\sqrt{1 - \epsilon^2}, 0, \epsilon), y = (-\sqrt{1 - \epsilon^2}, 0, \epsilon)$,
and $z = (0,\sqrt{1 - \epsilon^2}, \epsilon)$ with $\epsilon > 0$ small.
Then $x, y, z \in B(N, \arccos(\epsilon)) \subset B(N, \pi/2 - \epsilon)$ where $N = (0, 0, 1)$ denotes the north pole.
Let the barycenter of $\mu_a$ be $b_a$. A straightforward calculation
shows that $\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(N,
b_a) \geq \frac{ac}{\epsilon}$ so long
as $a \leq c' \epsilon$,
for constants $c,c' > 0$.
Let
$n = \lceil \frac1\epsilon \rceil$ and
$a =\frac{c'}{n}$,
and let $\widehat b_n$ denote the empirical barycenter
for a sample of size $n$ from $\mu_a$.
Then with
constant probability, the sample doesn't contain $z$ and $\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(N, \widehat b_n)
\leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}$.
On this event,
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(\widehat b_n, b_a) \geq 
\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(N, b_a) - \mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(N, \widehat b_n)
\geq \frac{ac}{\epsilon}-  \frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}.
$$ So long as $\epsilon$ is sufficiently small, this establishes
$\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(\widehat b_n, b_a)\gtrsim 1$ with constant probability.

Hence, any bound of the form $\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{S}^2}(\widehat b_n, b_a)^2]
\leq \frac{c_\epsilon}{n}$ must satisfy
$$
\frac{c_\epsilon}{n} \gtrsim 1 \implies c_\epsilon \gtrsim n \gtrsim \frac1\epsilon.
$$ 
Therefore, the blow-up of the constant in Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error} is necessary.

An analogous argument applies to the iterated barycenter $\widetilde b_n$, showing that the restriction $r<D_\kappa/2$ is necessary for such uniform bounds and that the constant in Theorem~\ref{th:iterated-expected-error} must also blow up as $r\uparrow D_\kappa/2$.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{def:barycentric-hull}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a metric space. The barycentric hull of a non-empty subset $A\subseteq\mathcal{X}$, denoted by $\mathrm{bar}(A)$, is the collection of all barycenters of probability measures finitely supported on $A$.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $A,B$ be two non-empty subsets of a bounded $\kappa$-domain of $\mathcal{X}$ of radius $r\geq0$.\footnote{If $\kappa>0$, all $\kappa$-domains are bounded by definition.}
Then,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{bar}(A),\mathrm{bar}(B))
&\leq
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\begin{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B), & \text{if }\kappa\leq0,\\
c_{\mathrm{bh,Lip}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B),
& \text{if }\kappa>0,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
    {\end{cases}}{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
where $c_{\mathrm{bh,Lip}}(u):=c_{W_1}(u)$ for every $u\in[0,\pi/2)$. Here, $c_{W_1}(u)$ is the constant appearing in Proposition~\ref{prop:wasserstein-one-stability}.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Let $\eta=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B)$ and fix $\varepsilon>0$. Set $c:=1$ if $\kappa\leq0$, and
$c:=c_{\mathrm{bh,Lip}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})$ if $\kappa>0$. Let $x\in \mathrm{bar}(A)$. That is, there exists a measure $\mu$ of the form $\mu=\sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i\delta_{x_i}$, for some integer $n\geq 1$, some weights $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_n\geq 0$ with $\omega_1+\ldots+\omega_n=1$ and some points $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in A$ such that $x=b(\mu)$. 
    For each $i=1,\ldots,n$, $\mathrm{d}(x_i,B)\leq \eta$ so one can find $y_i\in B$ with $\mathrm{d}(x_i,y_i)\leq\eta+\varepsilon$. Let $\nu=\sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i\delta_{y_i}$ and $y=b(\nu)$. Then, $y\in\mathrm{bar}(B)$. Using Proposition~\ref{prop:wasserstein-one-stability} if $\kappa>0$ or \cite[Theorem 6.3]{sturm2003} if $\kappa\leq 0$, we obtain that 
    $$\mathrm{d}(x,y)=\mathrm{d}(b(\mu),b(\nu))\leq c\,W_1(\mu,\nu).$$
    Now, choosing the coupling of $\mu$ and $\nu$ that simply pairs each $x_i$ with $y_i$, $i=1,\ldots,n$, we obtain 
    $$\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leq c\,\sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i \mathrm{d}(x_i,y_i)\leq c\,(\eta+\varepsilon).$$
    Since $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary, we thus have established
    that $\mathrm{d}(x,\mathrm{bar}(B))\leq c\eta$. Similarly, we can also obtain that for all $y\in \mathrm{bar}(B)$, $\mathrm{d}(y,\mathrm{bar}(A))\leq c\,\eta$, which then concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:Lip_conv_hulls}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $A,B$ be two non-empty subsets of a bounded $\kappa$-domain of $\mathcal{X}$ of radius $r\geq0$. Then,

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B))
&\leq

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B), & \text{if }\kappa\leq0,\\
c_{\mathrm{hull,Lip}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B),
& \text{if }\kappa>0,

    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax}

where, for every $u\in(0,\pi/2)$, $c_{\mathrm{hull,Lip}}(u):=u/(\sin u\cos u)$, understood as $1$ if $u=0$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
For every fixed $r>0$, $c_{\mathrm{hull,Lip}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\to1$ as $\kappa\downarrow0$, recovering the bound in nonpositively curved spaces.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:representation_conv_hull}
    Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a metric space and $A\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ be non-empty. Define the nested sequence of sets $(A_n)_{n\geq 0}$ inductively by setting $A_0=A$ and by letting, for all $n\geq 1$, $A_n$ be the collection of all points in $\mathcal{X}$ that are on a geodesic connecting two points of $A_{n-1}$. Then, 
    $$\mathrm{conv}(A)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0} A_n.$$
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:Jensen_conv}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a metric space and $A\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ be non-empty. Let $f:\mathcal{X}\to \mathbb R$ and assume that $f$ is geodesically convex on $\mathrm{conv}(A)$. Then, 
$$\sup_{x\in\mathrm{conv}(A)}f(x)\leq \sup_{x\in A}f(x).$$
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Using the notation of Lemma~\ref{lem:representation_conv_hull}, one can show, by induction, that for all $n\geq 1$ and all $x\in A_n$, $f(x)\leq\sup_{y\in A}f(y)$ and the conclusion follows immediately.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:conv_in_cone}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$, and let $D=B(o,r)$ be a $\kappa$-domain. Let $A\subseteq D$ be a non-empty set and let $C_0A=\{\lambda x:x\in A,\lambda\geq0\}$. We regard $C_0A$ as a subset of $C_0\mathcal{X}$, equipped with
the metric $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^{(\kappa)}$ from Definition~\ref{def:euclidean-cone}.
The following properties hold.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    {\begin{itemize}}{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \item $A$ is geodesically convex if and only if $C_0A$ is geodesically convex;
        \item $\mathrm{conv}(A)=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\exists\lambda>0,\, \lambda x\in\mathrm{conv}(C_0A)\}$. Likewise, radial projection of $\mathrm{conv}(C_0A)\setminus\{0\}$ onto the unit section gives $\{1\}\times\mathrm{conv}(A)$;
        \item $\mathrm{conv}(\{1\}\times A)$ only contains elements of the form $tx$ with $x\in D$ and $t\geq\cos(\sqrt{\kappa}\,r)$.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    {\end{itemize}}{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}$.
    The proof relies on the following fact, which can be found in the proof of \cite[Proposition I.5.10]{bridsonhaefliger1999}: Fix any two points $x,y\in D$ and any two positive numbers $s,u>0$. Let $\gamma:[0,1]\to \mathcal{X}$ be the unique geodesic from $x$ to $y$ in $\mathcal{X}$ and $\tilde\gamma:[0,1]\to C_0\mathcal{X}$ the unique geodesic from $sx$ to $uy$ in $C_0\mathcal{X}$. Then, $\gamma([0,1])=\{z\in D:\exists v>0,\ vz\in\tilde\gamma([0,1])\}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:geodesics-cone} gives a more precise statement). That is, geodesics between nonzero points in $C_0D$ project onto geodesics in $D$, while geodesics from the vertex are radial segments. This proves the first assertion. For the second, the projected hull is convex by the same geodesic argument and contains $A$, so it contains $\mathrm{conv}(A)$. Conversely, $C_0\mathrm{conv}(A)$ is convex by the first assertion and contains $C_0A$, so the projected hull is contained in $\mathrm{conv}(A)$.
    For the last part of the lemma, since $D$ is geodesically convex, the first part shows that $C_0D$ is geodesically convex. Hence $\mathrm{conv}(\{1\}\times A)\subseteq C_0D$. Now, let $\gamma:t\geq 0\mapsto to\in C_0D$ (recall that $o$ is the center of the ball $D$). It is clear that $\gamma$ is a geodesic ray, since $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))=|s-t|$ for all $s,t\geq 0$. Since $C_0\mathcal{X}$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space, the Busemann function $b_\gamma:\lambda x\in C_0\mathcal{X}\mapsto \lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl(\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\lambda x,\gamma(t))-t\bigr)$ associated with $\gamma$ is geodesically convex on $C_0\mathcal{X}$ (see \cite[Definition II.8.17]{bridsonhaefliger1999} for the definition -- geodesic convexity follows from the geodesic convexity of $\mathrm{d}$ in first argument). Now, one easily computes $b_\gamma(\lambda x)=-\lambda\cos(\mathrm{d}(x,o))$ for all $x\in D$. Now, for all $x\in A$, $b_\gamma(1x)=-\cos(\mathrm{d}(x,o))\leq -\cos(r)$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:Jensen_conv}, all elements $\lambda x\in \mathrm{conv}(\{1\}\times A)$ must satisfy $b_\gamma(\lambda x)\leq -\cos(r)$, that is, $-\lambda\cos(\mathrm{d}(x,o))\leq -\cos(r)$, implying that $\lambda\geq \cos(r)$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Let us first assume that $\kappa\leq 0$.
    Define two nested sequences of sets $(A_n)_{n\geq 0}$ and $(B_n)_{n\geq 0}$ inductively by setting $A_0=A$, $B_0=B$ and, for all $n\geq 1$, letting $A_n$ be the collection of all points in $\mathcal{X}$ that are on a geodesic connecting two points of $A_{n-1}$, and likewise for $B_n$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:representation_conv_hull}, $\mathrm{conv}(A)$ and $\mathrm{conv}(B)$ can be written as $\mathrm{conv}(A)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0} A_n$ and $\mathrm{conv}(B)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0} B_n$.
    Now, $\rho:=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B)$ and let us check that $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_n,B_n)\leq \rho$, by showing that the sequence $(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_n,B_n))_{n\geq 0}$ is non-increasing. Let $n\geq 1$ and fix $x\in A_n$. Let $x_1,x_2\in A_{n-1}$ be such that $x=\gamma(t)$ for some $t\in [0,1]$, where $\gamma$ is the geodesic from $x_1$ to $x_2$. Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and let $y_1,y_2\in B_{n-1}$ such that $\mathrm{d}(x_1,y_1)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$ and $\mathrm{d}(x_2,y_2)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$. Let $\gamma'$ be the geodesic from $y_1$ and $y_2$. Then, $y:=\gamma'(t)\in B_n$ by definition, so $\mathrm{d}(x,B_n)\leq \mathrm{d}(x,y)$. Since $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space and $\kappa\leq 0$,  $\mathrm{d}$ is jointly geodesically convex, yielding that $\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leq (1-t)\mathrm{d}(x_1,y_1)+t\mathrm{d}(x_2,y_2)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$. Therefore, $\mathrm{d}(x,B_n)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$. Similarly, for all $y\in B_n$, $\mathrm{d}(y,A_n)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$. Finally, we obtain the inequality $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_n,B_n)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})+\varepsilon$, which must hold for all $\varepsilon>0$, yielding $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_n,B_n)\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})$. The inequality $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B))\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B)$ then follows readily, since the sequences $(A_n)_{n\geq 0}$ and $(B_n)_{n\geq 0}$ are nested.
    
    
    Let us now consider the case $\kappa>0$ and, by a scaling
    argument, we assume that $\kappa=1$ without loss of generality.
    Let $o\in\mathcal{X}$ such that $A$ and $B$ are included in the ball
    $B(o,r)$. This ball is a $\mathrm{CAT}(1)$ space itself, so its Euclidean cone $C$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space by Berestovskii's cone theorem \cite[Theorem II.3.14]{bridsonhaefliger1999}. We identify $C=C_0D$, where $D=B(o,r)$, with a convex subset of $C_0\mathcal{X}$, equipped with the restriction of $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}$. Hausdorff distances between subsets of the cone are computed using this metric. Let $\tilde A=\{1x:x\in A\}$ and $\tilde B=\{1y:y\in B\}$. 
    
    Let $\pi:C_0\mathcal{X}\setminus \{0\}\to C_0\mathcal{X}$ be the map defined by setting $\pi(\lambda x)=1x$, for all $x\in \mathcal{X}$ and $\lambda>0$. 
    By the third part of Lemma~\ref{lem:conv_in_cone}, $\mathrm{conv}(\{1\}\times D)\subseteq C_0\mathcal{X}\setminus \{0\}$, so $\pi$ is well-defined on $\mathrm{conv}(\{1\}\times D)$. By the same geodesic projection argument as in the second part of Lemma~\ref{lem:conv_in_cone}, $\{1\}\times \mathrm{conv}(A)$ and $\{1\}\times \mathrm{conv}(B)$ can then be identified with $\pi(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde A))$ and $\pi(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde B))$ respectively. Moreover, one easily checks that $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\tilde A,\tilde B)=2\sin(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B)/2)$ and $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\{1\}\times \mathrm{conv}(A),\{1\}\times \mathrm{conv}(B))=2\sin(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B))/2)$, since $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(1x,1y)=2\sin(\mathrm{d}(x,y)/2)$ for all $x,y\in B(o,r)$. 
    Denote by $\rho=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(A,B)$ and $\rho'=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B))$. 

    By the first part of the proof (case $\kappa\leq 0$), we have that  
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_ch1}
        \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde A),\mathrm{conv}(\tilde B))\leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\tilde A,\tilde B)=2\sin(\rho/2).
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Moreover, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_ch2}
        2\sin(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B))/2)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\pi(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde A)),\pi(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde B))).
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Let us now show that this restriction is $1/\cos(r)$-Lipschitz. Fix $x_1,x_2\in D$ and $\lambda_1,\lambda_2\geq \cos(r)$. Then,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\pi(\lambda_1x_1),\pi(\lambda_2x_2))^2 & = \mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(1x_1,1x_2)^2 \\
        & = 2-2\cos(\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2)) \\
        & = \frac{1}{\lambda_1\lambda_2} \left(2\lambda_1\lambda_2-2\lambda_1\lambda_2\cos(\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2))\right) \\
        & \leq \frac{1}{\lambda_1\lambda_2} \left(\lambda_1^2+\lambda_2^2-2\lambda_1\lambda_2\cos(\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2))\right) \\
        & = \frac{1}{\lambda_1\lambda_2} \mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\lambda_1x_1,\lambda_2x_2)^2 \\
        & \leq \frac{1}{\cos(r)^2} \mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(\lambda_1x_1,\lambda_2x_2)^2
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we have used the third part of Lemma~\ref{lem:conv_in_cone} in the last inequality.
    Therefore, the right-hand side of \eqref{eqn:proof_ch2} is bounded from above by $(1/\cos(r))\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm H}(\mathrm{conv}(\tilde A),\mathrm{conv}(\tilde B))$. Together with \eqref{eqn:proof_ch1}, we obtain that 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_ch3}
        \sin(\rho'/2)\leq \frac{\sin(\rho/2)}{\cos(r)}.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Since $A,B,\mathrm{conv}(A),\mathrm{conv}(B)\subseteq B(o,r)$, we have $\rho,\rho'\leq2r$. For $r>0$, the inequalities
    \[
    \frac{\sin r}{r}\,u\leq\sin u\leq u,
    \qquad 0\leq u\leq r,
    \]
    together with \eqref{eqn:proof_ch3}, give
    \[
    \frac{\sin r}{2r}\rho'
    \leq\sin(\rho'/2)
    \leq\frac{\sin(\rho/2)}{\cos r}
    \leq\frac{\rho}{2\cos r}.
    \]
    Hence
    \[
    \rho'\leq\frac{r}{\sin r\cos r}\rho.
    \]
    The case $r=0$ is immediate.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:tangent-cone-dimension}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$.
Assume that $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ has curvature bounded from below.
Then, for every $x\in\mathcal{X}$, $C_x\mathcal{X}$ has the same Hausdorff dimension as $\mathcal{X}$,
and this common dimension is either a nonnegative integer or infinite.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:Yokota_Ohta}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a separable $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Assume that $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ has curvature bounded from below. Let $\mu$ be a Borel probability measure supported on a bounded $\kappa$-domain $D$ of $\mathcal{X}$,
and let $b\in D$ be its unique barycenter. Then:
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    {\begin{itemize}}{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \item The tangent cone $C_b\mathcal{X}$ contains a subset $H$ isometric to a Hilbert space for the induced cone metric, with its origin identified with $0_b$ and $\log_b(\operatorname{supp}(\mu))\subseteq H$;
    \item For every $u\in C_b\mathcal{X}$,
    $\int_D\langle u,\log_b(x)\rangle_b\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0$.
    In particular, $\int_D\log_b(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0_b$ in $H$;
    \item Any $b'\in D$ satisfying $\int_D\langle u,\log_{b'}(x)\rangle_{b'}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0$ for every $u\in C_{b'}\mathcal{X}$ is equal to $b$.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
    {\end{itemize}}{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:classic_carat}
    Let $d\geq 1$ be an integer and let $A\subseteq \mathbb R^d$ be non-empty. Then, for all $x\in \mathrm{conv}(A)$, there exists $x_1,\ldots,x_{d+1}\in A$ with $x\in\mathrm{conv}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_{d+1}\})$.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:Carathéodory}

Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and with curvature bounded from below. Assume that $\mathcal{X}$ has finite Hausdorff dimension $d\geq 1$. Let $A\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ be a non-empty set included in a $\kappa$-domain. Then, for all $x\in\mathrm{bar}(A)$, there exists a measure $\mu$ with finite support of size at most $d+1$, included in $A$, whose barycenter is $x$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let $x\in\mathrm{bar}(A)$. That is, there exist $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in A$ and weights $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n\geq 0$ with $\lambda_1+\ldots+\lambda_n=1$ such that $x$ is the unique minimizer of $y\in\mathcal{X}\mapsto\frac12\sum_{i=1}^n\lambda_i\mathrm{d}(y,x_i)^2$. After discarding zero weights, we may assume that $\lambda_i>0$ for every $i$. If $\kappa\leq0$, the finite set $\{x,x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$ is contained in a bounded $\kappa$-domain, so Theorem~\ref{thm:Yokota_Ohta} applies. By Theorem~\ref{thm:Yokota_Ohta}, 
    $$0=\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i\log_x(x_i).$$
    That is, if $H$ is as in Theorem~\ref{thm:Yokota_Ohta}, $0$ is in the convex hull of $\log_x(x_1),\ldots,\log_x(x_n)$. Moreover, by Theorem~\ref{thm:Yokota_Ohta}, $H$ is isometrically embedded in $C_x\mathcal{X}$, whose Hausdorff dimension equals $d$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:tangent-cone-dimension}. Thus $\dim H\leq d$, and Carathéodory's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:classic_carat}) yields that one can extract at most $d+1$ vectors from $\{\log_x(x_1),\ldots,\log_x(x_n)\}$ whose convex hull in $H$ contains $0_x$, that is, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_metric_carat_181}
        0_x=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\omega_i \log_x(y_i)
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for some elements $y_1,\ldots,y_{d+1}\in\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$ and some weights $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_{d+1}\geq0$ satisfying $\omega_1+\ldots+\omega_{d+1}=1$. 
    Denote by $\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\omega_i \delta_{\log_x(y_i)}$. Then, $\nu \in \mathcal P_2(C_x\mathcal{X})$ and it satisfies $\operatorname{supp}(\nu)\subseteq H$. Since $C_x\mathcal{X}$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space, $\nu$ has a unique barycenter $b(\nu)$. Moreover, since $H$ is isometric to a Hilbert space, it is a closed, geodesically convex subset of $C_x\mathcal{X}$. Therefore, since $C_x\mathcal{X}$ is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space,
    \cite[Proposition~6.1]{sturm2003} gives $b(\nu)\in H$. Now, \eqref{eqn:proof_metric_carat_181} yields that $0_x$ is the minimizer of $F_\nu$ on $H$ and, therefore, $b(\nu)=0_x$. Hence, letting $\mu_0:=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\omega_i\delta_{y_i}$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:dual-norm-tangent-cone}, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \sup_{u\in C_x\mathcal{X}, |u|_x\leq 1}
    \int_{\mathcal{X}}\langle u,\log_x(y)\rangle_x\,\mathrm{d}\mu_0(y)
    =
    \sup_{u\in C_x\mathcal{X}, |u|_x\leq 1}
    \int_{C_x\mathcal{X}}\langle u,v\rangle_x\,\mathrm{d}\nu(v)
    =0.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    so Proposition~\ref{prop:barycenter-tangent-process} yields that $\mathrm{d}(x,b(\mu_0))=0$, which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a finite-dimensional $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$ with curvature bounded from below. Let $A\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ be a compact set that is included in some $\kappa$-domain. Then, $\mathrm{bar}(A)$ is compact.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Set $d:=\dim_{\mathrm H}(\mathcal{X})$, and let $D$ be a $\kappa$-domain containing $A$.
    Let $(x_n)_{n\geq1}$ be a sequence of elements of $\mathrm{bar}(A)$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:Carathéodory}, for each $n\geq1$, one can find $y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{d+1,n}\in A$ and weights $\lambda_{1,n},\ldots,\lambda_{d+1,n}\geq0$ with $\lambda_{1,n}+\ldots+\lambda_{d+1,n}=1$ such that $x_n$ is the unique minimizer of the map $F_n(y):=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}
\lambda_{i,n}\mathrm{d}(y,y_{i,n})^2$.
    The $d+2$ sequences $(y_{1,n})_{n\geq1},\ldots,(y_{d+1,n})_{n\geq1}$ and $((\lambda_{1,n},\ldots,\lambda_{d+1,n}))_{n\geq1}$ all take values in compact sets ($A$ for the first $d+1$ and the $d$-dimensional Euclidean simplex for the last), so one can extract a common converging subsequence.
    Up to renumbering, let us then simply assume that all $d+2$ sequences converge, and denote by $y_1,\ldots,y_{d+1}$ and $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{d+1})$ their limits.
    Let $F(y):=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\lambda_i\mathrm{d}(y,y_i)^2$.
    Since $y_1,\ldots,y_{d+1}$ are all in $A$, the measure $\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\lambda_i\delta_{y_i}$ is supported on $D$, so its barycenter $b^*$ is the unique minimizer of $F$ on $\mathcal{X}$ and belongs to $D$.

    Moreover, $F_n$ converges uniformly to $F$ on all bounded subsets of $\mathcal{X}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:bary_in_convex_hull}, all $x_n$ and $b^*$ belong to the bounded set $C:=\overline{\mathrm{conv}(A)}\subseteq D$.
    By the variance inequality, Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic-variance-cat-k},\[
    c_{\mathrm{var}}(r\sqrt{\kappa})\,\mathrm{d}^2(x_n,b^*)
    \leq F(x_n)-F(b^*)
    \leq 2\sup_{y\in C}|F_n(y)-F(y)|
    \longrightarrow0,
    \]
    where $r$ is the radius of $D$ if $\kappa>0$; for $\kappa\leq0$, the coefficient on the left-hand side is replaced by $1/2$, by \cite[Proposition~4.4]{sturm2003}. The second inequality uses $F_n(x_n)\leq F_n(b^*)$.
    Thus $x_n\to b^*\in\mathrm{bar}(A)$, which proves compactness of $\mathrm{bar}(A)$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{th:sparse_approximation}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space for some $\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and let $A\subseteq\mathcal{X}$ be a non-empty set included in a bounded $\kappa$-domain of radius $r$. Fix $\varepsilon>0$.
Then, for every $x\in\mathrm{bar}(A)$, there exist $x_1,\ldots,x_N\in A$ with $N\leq\max\{1,\lceil
c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa)\textrm{rad}(A)^2/\varepsilon^2 \rceil\}$ such that
$\mathrm{d}(x,\mathrm{bar}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_N\}))\leq\varepsilon$, where $c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa):=c_{\mathrm{emp}}(r,\kappa)$, with $c_{\mathrm{emp}}$ defined in Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error}.
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The proof relies on Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error} together with the probabilistic method. Let $x\in \mathrm{bar}(A)$. That is, there is some probability measure $\mu$ on $\mathcal{X}$ with (finite) support included in $A$, whose (unique) barycenter is $x$. Now, let $n\geq 1$ be any positive integer and let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be i.i.d random variables with distribution $\mu$. Set $\sigma_\mu^2:=\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}^2(X_1,x)]$. By Theorem~\ref{th:empirical-expected-error}, the barycenter $\widehat b_n$ of $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ must satisfy $\mathbb E[\mathrm{d}(\widehat b_n,x)^2]\leq c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa)\sigma_\mu^2/n$. Therefore, with positive probability, $\mathrm{d}(\widehat b_n,x)^2\leq c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa)\sigma_\mu^2/n$. Moreover, all $X_i$'s, $i=1,\ldots,n$, belong to $A$ almost surely, since $\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq A$. Therefore, there exists a realization of $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in A$ whose barycenter is within distance $\sqrt{c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa)\sigma_\mu^2/n}$ of $x$. Now, since $x$ minimizes the Fréchet function $F_\mu$ of $\mu$, we have that, for any choice $a\in A$, 
    $$\sigma_\mu^2 = 2F_\mu(x) \leq 2F_\mu(a)=\int_{\mathcal{X}} \mathrm{d}^2(a,y)\mathrm{d}\mu(y) \leq \sup_{y\in A}\mathrm{d}^2(a,y)$$
    since $\mathrm{d}(a,\cdot)\leq \sup_{y\in A}\mathrm{d}(a,y)$ $\mu$-almost surely. Taking the infimum over $a\in A$, we obtain that $\sigma_\mu^2\leq\textrm{rad}(A)^2$.
    Choosing $n:=\max\{1,\lceil c_{\mathrm{Car}}(r,\kappa)\mathrm{rad}(A)^2/\varepsilon^2\rceil\}$, we obtain that $x$ is $\varepsilon$-close to the barycenter of at most $n$ points of $A$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{thm:bary=conv}
    Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d})$ be either a Euclidean space, a sphere or a hyperbolic space and $A\subseteq \mathcal{X}$ be non-empty. If $\mathcal{X}$ is a sphere, assume that $A$ is contained in an open hemisphere. Then, $\mathrm{bar}(A)=\mathrm{conv}(A)$.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
When $\mathcal{X}$ is a Euclidean space, the result is classical. When $\mathcal{X}$ is a sphere, the result for finite sets has been shown in \cite[Theorem~7]{bussfillmore2001}, and the general case follows by taking unions over finite subsets of $A$. It stems from the fact that any geodesically convex subset of an open hemisphere can be written as the intersection of that hemisphere with a Euclidean convex cone (in the ambient Euclidean space). Let us focus on hyperbolic spaces. Without loss of generality, let us use their Beltrami-Klein representation. That is, let $\mathcal{X}$ be the open, Euclidean, unit ball, equipped with the metric
    $$\mathrm{d}(x,y)=\mathrm{arccosh}\left(\frac{1-x^\top y}{\sqrt{(1-\|x\|^2)(1-\|y\|^2)}}\right), \quad x,y\in\mathcal{X}.$$
    where $x^\top y$ stands for the Euclidean inner product and $\|\cdot\|$ is the Euclidean norm. Then, geodesics in $\mathcal{X}$ are simply line segments, parameterized with non-constant Euclidean speed \cite[Proposition 5.28]{lee2018introduction}. Therefore, geodesically convex sets are exactly those subsets of $\mathcal{X}$ that are convex for the Euclidean geometry. Now, let $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in\mathcal{X}$, for some $n\geq1$. The case $n=1$ is immediate, so assume $n\geq2$. Let us show that $\mathrm{conv}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})=\mathrm{bar}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$, which will yield the desired result. Let $y\in\mathrm{bar}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$. That is, by the first order condition, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof_hyper1}
        \sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i \log_y(x_i)=0
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for some weights $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_n\geq 0$ with $\omega_1+\ldots+\omega_n=1$. We identify $T_y\mathcal{X}$ with $\mathbb R^d$ through the Klein coordinates. Using again the characterization of geodesics in $\mathcal{X}$ as
    line segments, we have that $\log_y(x_i)=\lambda_i(x_i-y)$ for some $\lambda_i\geq 0$ that depends on $y$ and $x_i$. When $x_i\neq y$, $\lambda_i>0$ while when $x_i=y$, $\lambda_i$ can be chosen arbitrarily -- hence, we assume that all $\lambda_i$'s are positive. \eqref{eqn:proof_hyper1} then becomes 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\begin{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   
        \sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i\lambda_i (x_i-y)=0
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    which implies that $y$ belongs to the Euclidean convex hull
    of $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, and hence that $y\in\mathrm{conv}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$. This shows that $\mathrm{bar}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})\subseteq \mathrm{conv}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$.
    
    Now, let $y\in \mathrm{conv}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$. Then, $y$ is in the Euclidean convex hull of $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, so $y$ must be a convex combination of those points, that is, 
    $$y=\sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i x_i$$
    for some $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n\geq0$ with $\alpha_1+\ldots+\alpha_n=1$. That is, 
    $$\sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i(x_i-y)=0.$$
    For $i=1,\ldots,n$, let $\tilde\omega_i=\alpha_i/\lambda_i$ and $\omega_i=\tilde\omega_i/(\sum_{j=1}^n\tilde\omega_j)$; where $\lambda_i$ is the same as defined above, so $\log_y(x_i)=\lambda_i(x_i-y)$. Then, 
    $$\sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i\lambda_i(x_i-y)=0$$
    that is,
    $$\sum_{i=1}^n \omega_i\log_{y}(x_i)=0$$
    which ensures that $y$ is the barycenter of the probability
    measure $\sum_{i=1}^n\omega_i\delta_{x_i}$, since every critical
    point of the Fr\'echet function is a minimizer in the hyperbolic space.
    That is, $y\in\mathrm{bar}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})$. Since every point of either hull belongs to the corresponding
    hull of a finite subset of $A$, this proves the general case.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. Since $D$ has diameter strictly smaller than $\pi$,
all three distances are smaller than $\pi$,
and $\sin\mathrm d(x,y)\sin\mathrm d(x,z)>0$.

If $\mathrm d(x,y)+\mathrm d(x,z)+\mathrm d(y,z)<2\pi$, the spherical law of cosines and
Proposition~\ref{prop:angle-comparison} give
\[
\frac{
\cos\mathrm d(y,z)-\cos\mathrm d(x,y)\cos\mathrm d(x,z)
}{
\sin\mathrm d(x,y)\sin\mathrm d(x,z)
}
=
\cos\angle_x^{(1)}(y,z)
\leq\cos\angle_x(y,z).
\]

Otherwise, if $0<2\pi-\mathrm d(x,y)-\mathrm d(x,z)\leq\mathrm d(y,z)<\pi$, then by the monotonicity of $\cos$ on $[0,\pi]$,
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\cos\mathrm d(y,z)
&\leq\cos\bigl(2\pi-\mathrm d(x,y)-\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr)\\
&=\cos\bigl(\mathrm d(x,y)+\mathrm d(x,z)\bigr)\\
&=\cos\mathrm d(x,y)\cos\mathrm d(x,z)
-\sin\mathrm d(x,y)\sin\mathrm d(x,z).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Consequently,
\[
\frac{
\cos\mathrm d(y,z)-\cos\mathrm d(x,y)\cos\mathrm d(x,z)
}{
\sin\mathrm d(x,y)\sin\mathrm d(x,z)
}
\leq-1
\leq\cos\angle_x(y,z).
\]
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-17: body_md -->
\label{prop:geodesics-cone}
Let $(\mathcal{X},\mathrm d)$ be a $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ space, with $\kappa>0$, and let $C_0\mathcal{X}$ be its Euclidean cone. Let $y,z\in\mathcal{X}$ satisfy $\mathrm d(y,z)<D_\kappa$, and let $\gamma\in\Gamma(y,z)$.
Let $\alpha_t, \beta_t$ be as defined in
Proposition~\ref{prop:geodesic-cosine-inequality}.
Then the unique geodesic $q$ in $(C_0\mathcal{X},\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^{(\kappa)})$ from $1y$ to $1z$ satisfies, for every $t\in[0,1]$,
\[
q\left(\frac{\beta_t}{\alpha_t+\beta_t}\right)
=
\frac{1}{\alpha_t+\beta_t}\,\gamma(t).
\]
<!-- end proposition-17 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. The case $y=z$ is immediate, so assume $0<\mathrm d(y,z)<\pi$. Choose $\bar y,\bar z\in\mathbb S^2$ such that
$\mathrm d_{\mathbb S^2}(\bar y,\bar z)=\mathrm d(y,z)$, and let $\bar\gamma\in\Gamma(\bar y,\bar z)$.
Then, for all $s,t\in[0,1]$,
\[
\mathrm d(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))
=|s-t|\mathrm d(y,z)
=\mathrm d_{\mathbb S^2}(\bar\gamma(s),\bar\gamma(t)).
\]
The spherical interpolation formula \cite{shoemake1985} gives
\[
\bar\gamma(t)=\alpha_t\bar y+\beta_t\bar z.
\]
Since the Euclidean cone over $\mathbb S^2$ can be isometrically identified with $\mathbb R^3$, its geodesic joining $\bar y$ and $\bar z$ is simply the segment $\bar q(s)=(1-s)\bar y + s \bar z, s\in [0,1]$. Thus, setting $\sigma(t) :=\beta_t/(\alpha_t+\beta_t)$ and $\rho_t:=1/(\alpha_t+\beta_t)$,
we obtain 
\[
\bar q(\sigma(t))
=\rho_t\,\bar\gamma(t).
\]
This proves the formula in the spherical case and explains its origin. We now transfer it to the $\mathrm{CAT}(1)$ case.
Differentiating $\sigma$, one sees that it is strictly increasing and
therefore is a bijection from $[0,1]$ onto itself. We may therefore define a path $\widetilde\gamma:[0,1]\to C_0\mathcal{X}$ by setting 
\[
\widetilde\gamma(\sigma(t))
:=\rho_t\,\gamma(t), t\in [0,1].
\]
By the cone distance formula and the equality of distances along $\gamma$ and $\bar\gamma$, we have
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
&\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}\bigl(
\widetilde\gamma(\sigma(t)),
\widetilde\gamma(\sigma(s))\bigr)\\
&\qquad=
\|\bar q(\sigma(t))-\bar q(\sigma(s))\|_{\mathbb R^3}\\
&\qquad=
|\sigma(t)-\sigma(s)|\,\|\bar y-\bar z\|_{\mathbb R^3}\\
&\qquad=
|\sigma(t)-\sigma(s)|\,\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}(1y,1z).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Hence $\widetilde\gamma$ is a geodesic joining $1y$ to $1z$. Uniqueness of geodesics in $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space therefore gives $\widetilde\gamma=q$, proving the claim.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. The case $y=z$ is immediate, so assume $y\neq z$. When $\mathrm d(x,y)+\mathrm d(y,z)+\mathrm d(x,z)<2\pi$, a proof using a comparison triangle is straightforward, by the $\mathrm{CAT}(1)$ inequality and the spherical law of cosines (see~\cite[Corollary~2.2]{KimuraSato2012}). However, as for the angle comparison in Proposition~\ref{prop:cosine-comparison-domain}, we want to remove this perimeter assumption.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  

To do so, we will use the Euclidean cone $C_0\mathcal{X}$, which, equipped with $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}$ (recall that we assume that $\kappa=1$ here), is a $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ space, and, hence, whose squared distance to any given point is $2$-geodesically convex, by \cite[Proposition~2.3]{sturm2003}.

Let $q$ be the geodesic joining $1y$ to $1z$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:geodesics-cone}, we have $q(\sigma(t))=\rho_t\gamma(t)$ with $\sigma(t) =\beta_t/(\alpha_t+\beta_t)$ and $\rho_t=1/(\alpha_t+\beta_t)$, for all $t\in [0,1]$.
Using the strong geodesic convexity of $\mathrm{d}_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(1x,\cdot)$,
\[
\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(1x,\rho_t\gamma(t)) \leq (1-\sigma(t))\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(1x,1y) + \sigma(t)\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(1x,1z) - \sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))\mathrm d_{C_0\mathcal{X}}^2(1y,1z).
\]
All distances between points of $D$ are less than $\pi$. Expanding the cone distances therefore gives
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
1+\rho_t^2-2\rho_t\cos\mathrm d(x,\gamma(t))
&\leq
2-2(1-\sigma(t))\cos\mathrm d(x,y)-2\sigma(t)\cos\mathrm d(x,z)\\
&\quad \quad \quad \quad \quad -2\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(y,z)\bigr).
  
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
One can check that $\alpha_t^2+\beta_t^2 +2\alpha_t\beta_t\cos\mathrm d(y,z)=1$, either using trigonometric formulas, or using the fact that, in the Euclidean space $\mathbb R^3$, $\alpha_t \bar y+\beta_t \bar z\in\mathbb S^2$, so $\alpha_t^2+\beta_t^2 +2\alpha_t\beta_t\cos\mathrm d(y,z)=\|\alpha_t\bar y+\beta_t\bar z\|_{\mathbb R^3}^2=1$, where $\bar y,\bar z\in\mathbb S^2$ are comparison points on $\mathbb S^2$ for $y$ and $z$, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:geodesics-cone}. Dividing by $(\alpha_t+\beta_t)^2$, we obtain
\[
1+\rho_t^2
=
2-2\sigma(t)(1-\sigma(t))
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(y,z)\bigr).
\]
Substituting this equality into the preceding inequality and cancelling the common terms gives
\[
\rho_t\cos\mathrm d(x,\gamma(t))
\geq
(1-\sigma(t))\cos\mathrm d(x,y)
+\sigma(t)\cos\mathrm d(x,z).
\]
Finally, dividing by $\rho_t$ yields the desired inequality.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$. The case $t=1$ is immediate. If $x=z$, then $\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))=(1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,y)$, so the result follows. We therefore assume that $x\neq z$. Finally, assume that $y\in D\setminus\{x\}$ is close enough to $x$ so $y\neq z$ and $\mathrm{d}(x,y)+\mathrm{d}(x,z)+\mathrm{d}(y,z)<2\pi$ (this is possible, since the left hand side goes to $2\mathrm{d}(x,z)$ as $y\to x$, and $2\mathrm{d}(x,z)\leq 4r<2\pi$ by assumption). 
    
    For $t\in [0,1)$, let $\alpha(t)=\angle_z^{(1)}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))$. By \cite[Proposition II.3.1]{bridsonhaefliger1999}, $\alpha(t)$ is a non-increasing function of $t$. Moreover, as seen in Definition~\ref{def:comparison_angles}, we have, for $t\in [0,1)$,

   \label{eqn:proof_step254}
        \cos(\alpha(t))= \frac{\cos(\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t)))-\cos((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\cos((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))}{\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))}.

    Rearranging \eqref{eqn:proof_step254}, we obtain that for all $t\in [0,1)$,

    1 -\cos(\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))) & = 1-\cos((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\cos((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z)) \nonumber \\
    & \qquad -\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))\cos(\alpha(t)) \nonumber \\
    & = 1-\cos((1-t)(\mathrm{d}(x,z)-\mathrm{d}(y,z))) \nonumber \\
    & \qquad +\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))(1-\cos(\alpha(t))) \nonumber \\
    & = 2\sin^2\left(\frac{1-t}{2}(\mathrm{d}(x,z)-\mathrm{d}(y,z))\right) \label{eqn:proof_54231} \\
    & \qquad +2\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))\sin^2(\alpha(t)/2) \nonumber \\
    & \leq 2\sin^2\left(\frac{1-t}{2}(\mathrm{d}(x,z)-\mathrm{d}(y,z))\right) \label{eqn:proof_5423} \\
    & \qquad +2\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))\sin^2(\alpha(0)/2) \nonumber
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {

where the last line makes use of the inequality $0\leq \alpha(t)/2\leq \alpha(0)/2\leq \pi/2$.

Applying the equality \eqref{eqn:proof_54231} with $t=0$ for the denominator and the inequality \eqref{eqn:proof_5423} for the numerator, and letting
$\beta_y:=\mathrm{d}(x,z)-\mathrm{d}(y,z)$, we obtain that

    \, & \frac{1-\cos(\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t)))}{1-\cos(\mathrm{d}(x,y))} \\

Since all summands are nonnegative, the right-hand side
is bounded by
\[
\max\left\{
\frac{\sin^2((1-t)\beta_y/2)}{\sin^2(\beta_y/2)},
\frac{\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(y,z))}
     {\sin(\mathrm{d}(x,z))\sin(\mathrm{d}(y,z))}
\right\},
\]
where the first quotient is understood as $(1-t)^2$ when $\beta_y=0$. As $y\to x$, these quotients converge respectively to $(1-t)^2$ and $\sin^2((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))/\sin^2(\mathrm{d}(x,z))$.
By concavity of $\sin$ on $[0,\pi]$,
\[
\sin((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))
\geq(1-t)\sin(\mathrm{d}(x,z)),
\]
so the second limit is at least the first.
Taking the limsup therefore gives

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   \label{eqn:proof98765}
    \limsup_{\substack{y\to x \\ y\in D\setminus\{x\}}} \frac{1-\cos(\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t)))}{1-\cos(\mathrm{d}(x,y))} \leq  \frac{\sin^2((1-t)\mathrm{d}(x,z))}{\sin^2(\mathrm{d}(x,z))}

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

We conclude by noting that, as $y\to x$, both $\mathrm{d}(x,y)$ and $\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))\to 0$ (geodesics vary continuously with their endpoints in $\kappa$-domains of $\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)$ spaces, $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, see \cite[Proposition II.1.4]{bridsonhaefliger1999}), hence, the left-hand side of \eqref{eqn:proof98765} coincides with $\displaystyle \limsup_{\substack{y\to x \\ y\in D\setminus\{x\}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\gamma(t),\eta(t))^2}{\mathrm{d}(x,y)^2}$, by $1-\cos u\sim u^2/2$ as $u\to0$.
Taking square roots proves the result.
