<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$.

For the first part of the lemma, we provide a more straightforward proof than the one in \cite[Proposition II.2.4(1)--(3) and Exercise II.2.6(1)]{bridsonhaefliger1999}, \cite[Proposition 3.1(i)]{ohta2007}. Denote by $\rho:=\mathrm{d}(x,C)$ and take an arbitrary $\varepsilon\in(0,\pi/2-\rho)$.
It is easy to see that finding a projection amounts to minimizing
\[
z\in C\cap B(x,\rho+\varepsilon)\longmapsto d^2(x,z).
\] 
The set $C \cap B(x,\rho+\varepsilon)$ is nonempty and convex, as an intersection of convex sets. It is also complete, since it is closed in the complete space $\mathcal{X}$. By spherical comparison, as in the proof of \cite[Proposition 3.1(i)]{ohta2007}, the fixed-center function $z \in B(x,\rho+\varepsilon) \mapsto d^2(x,z) $ is $\alpha(\varepsilon)-$strongly convex, with $\alpha(\varepsilon)>0$. Thus, since the function is continuous, we can apply \cite[Proposition 1.7]{sturm2003} to obtain a unique minimizer $p$ satisfying $d(x,p)=\rho$. Finally, any minimizer on $C$ belongs to
$C\cap B(x,\rho+\varepsilon)$, which proves the existence and uniqueness of the projection $p_C(x)=p$.

For completeness, let us now provide the proof of the obtuse angle property.
Assume $x\notin C$ and denote $p:=p_C(x)$. By contradiction, assume that there exists $c\in C\setminus\{p\}$ such that
\[
\angle_p(x,c)<\frac{\pi}{2}.
\]
Since the spherical comparison angles converge to the Alexandrov angle as both points approach $p$, we may choose $x'\in[p,x]\setminus\{p\}$ and $c'\in[p,c]\setminus\{p\}$ sufficiently close to $p$ such that the triangle $\Delta(p,x',c')$ has perimeter less than $2\pi$ and
\[
\alpha:=\angle_p^{(1)}(x',c')<\frac{\pi}{2}.
\]
Consider its spherical comparison triangle $\Delta(\bar p,\bar x',\bar c')$ and denote
$a:=\mathrm d(p,x')\in(0,\pi/2)$. For $0<h<\mathrm d(p,c')$, let $c_h\in[p,c']$ and $\bar c_h\in[\bar p,\bar c']$ be the corresponding points satisfying
\[
\mathrm d(p,c_h)=\mathrm d_1(\bar p,\bar c_h)=h.
\]
Since $C$ is geodesically convex, $c_h\in C$. For an illustration of this construction, see Figure~\ref{fig:chebyshev-projection}. Since $\bar c_h\in[\bar p,\bar c']$, the angle at $\bar p$ between $[\bar p,\bar x']$ and $[\bar p,\bar c_h]$ is $\alpha$.
By the spherical law of cosines, we get
\[
\cos\mathrm d_1(\bar x',\bar c_h)
=\cos a\cos h+\sin a\sin h\cos\alpha.
\]
Hence,
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\cos\mathrm d_1(\bar x',\bar c_h)-\cos a
&=\cos a(\cos h-1)+\sin a\sin h\cos\alpha\\
&=\sin h\left[
\sin a\cos\alpha-\cos a\tan\left(\frac h2\right)
\right].
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Since $\sin a\cos\alpha>0$, the right-hand side is positive for sufficiently small $h>0$.
Therefore, $\mathrm d_1(\bar x',\bar c_h)<a$. By the $\mathrm{CAT}(1)$ comparison and the triangle inequality,
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\mathrm d(x,c_h)
&\leq\mathrm d(x,x')+\mathrm d(x',c_h)\\
&\leq\mathrm d(x,x')+\mathrm d_1(\bar x',\bar c_h)\\
&<\mathrm d(x,x')+\mathrm d(x',p)=\mathrm d(x,p).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Since $c_h\in C$, this contradicts the definition of $p=p_C(x)$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\kappa>0$. The case $z=x$ is immediate, so assume $z\neq x$.For $y=x$, the squared-distance derivative is zero. For every $y\in D\setminus\{x\}$, we have $\mathrm d(x,y),\mathrm d(x,z)<D_\kappa$, so the first variation formula~\eqref{eq:first-variation} gives
\[
\lim_{t\downarrow0}
\frac{\frac12\mathrm d^2(\gamma_{xz}(t),y)-\frac12\mathrm d^2(x,y)}{t}
=
-\big\langle \log_x y,\log_x z\big\rangle_x.
\]
Since $\mu$ has bounded support, the difference quotients are uniformly bounded by an integrable constant. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem,
\[
DF_\mu(x)[\log_x z]
=
-\int_{D}
\langle \log_x y,\log_x z\rangle_x
\,\mathrm d\mu(y).
\]
To obtain the extension to the ambient tangent cone, fix $x\in D$ and consider any nonconstant geodesic $\eta$ issuing from $x$. Since $\mathrm d(x,y)\leq2r<D_\kappa$ for every $y\in D$, the same first variation and dominated convergence argument applies along a sufficiently short initial segment of $\eta$. Allowing arbitrary nonnegative speeds therefore defines
\[
DF_\mu(x)[v]
=
-\int_D\langle\log_x y,v\rangle_x\,\mathrm d\mu(y),
\qquad v\in\widetilde C_x\mathcal{X}.
\]
For every $\varepsilon>0$, the function $F_\mu$ is $(2r+\varepsilon)$-Lipschitz on $B(x,\varepsilon)$. Let $v,w\in\widetilde C_x\mathcal{X}$ be represented by geodesics
$\eta_v,\eta_w$ issuing from $x$, defined near $0$ with respective constant speeds $|v|_x$ and $|w|_x$. For sufficiently small $t>0$,
\[
\left|
\frac{F_\mu(\eta_v(t))-F_\mu(x)}{t}
-
\frac{F_\mu(\eta_w(t))-F_\mu(x)}{t}
\right|
\leq
(2r+\varepsilon)
\frac{\mathrm d(\eta_v(t),\eta_w(t))}{t}.
\]
By the definition of the Alexandrov angle in Definition~\ref{def:alexandrov-angle} and the Euclidean comparison angle (see Remark~\ref{rem:euclidean-angle}),
\[
\lim_{t\downarrow0}
\frac{\mathrm d(\eta_v(t),\eta_w(t))}{t}
=
\mathrm d_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(v,w).
\]
Letting $t\downarrow0$ and then $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, we obtain
\[
|DF_\mu(x)[v]-DF_\mu(x)[w]|
\leq2r\,\mathrm d_{C_x\mathcal{X}}(v,w).
\]
Thus, the extension theorem for uniformly continuous maps
\cite[Chapter~XI, Section~3, Theorem~1]{schwartz_analyse2}
gives a unique continuous extension of $DF_\mu(x)$ to $C_x\mathcal{X}$. Passing to the limit in the preceding inequality shows that this extension remains $2r$-Lipschitz.
The integral representation extends by continuity of the pseudo-inner product and dominated convergence.
 
If $\kappa\leq0$, the space is $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ and the proof is similar, without a bounded-support assumption.
Indeed, for $\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathcal{X})$, $\gamma\in\Gamma(x,z)$ and $h:=\mathrm{d}(x,z)$,
\[
\left|
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2(\gamma(t),y)-\mathrm{d}^2(x,y)}{2t}
\right|
\leq h\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)+\frac12h^2,
\qquad 0<t\leq1.
\]
The right-hand side is integrable, so dominated convergence gives the same directional derivative formula. The extension to $C_x\mathcal{X}$ follows as above, with $2r$
replaced by $M_x:=\int_\mathcal{X}\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)<\infty$: the function $F_\mu$ is $(M_x+\varepsilon)$-Lipschitz on $B(x,\varepsilon)$, and its directional derivative
therefore extends to an $M_x$-Lipschitz function on $C_x\mathcal{X}$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
Let $(M,\mathrm d)$ be a complete metric space, and let $f:M\to\mathbb R$ be lower semicontinuous and bounded below.
Let $\varepsilon>0$ and $x_0\in M$ satisfy
\[
f(x_0)<\inf_{x\in M}f(x)+\varepsilon.
\]
Then there exists $x_\varepsilon\in M$ such that
\[
f(x_\varepsilon)\leq f(x_0),
\qquad
\mathrm d(x_0,x_\varepsilon)<1,
\]
and, for every $y\in M$,
\[
f(y)\geq f(x_\varepsilon)
-\varepsilon\,\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,y).
\]
<!-- end lemma-9 -->

F_\mu(y)\geq F_\mu(x_\varepsilon)
-\varepsilon\,\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,y).

<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:first-localization}
For every $\alpha>0$, there exists $a_\alpha>0$ such that
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

F_\mu(x)>
\inf_{z\in B(o,r)}F_\mu(z)+a_\alpha
\qquad
\text{for every }x\notin B(o,r+\alpha).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:second-localization}
For every $\rho\in(r,D_\kappa/2)$, there exists $a_\rho>0$ such that
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:negative-radial-derivative}
DF_\mu(x)\left[\frac{\log_x o}{\mathrm d(x,o)}\right]
<-a_\rho
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
for every
\[
x\in B(o,D_\kappa/2)\setminus B(o,\rho).
\]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:localization-approximate-minimizers}
For every $\rho\in(r,D_\kappa/2)$,
$x_\varepsilon\in B(o,\rho)$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$.
Fix $\rho\in(r,\pi/2)$ and choose $\alpha>0$ such that $\rho<r+\alpha<\pi/2$.
By \eqref{eq:def-xeps} and Yokota's first localization (Lemma~\ref{lem:first-localization}), we have $x_\varepsilon\in B(o,r+\alpha)$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:dir-derivative}, applied in this enlarged ball, justifies the directional derivative.
If $x_\varepsilon=o$, the conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise, differentiating \eqref{eq:ekeland-condition} along the geodesic towards $o$ gives
\[
DF_\mu(x_\varepsilon)
\left[\frac{\log_{x_\varepsilon}o}{\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,o)}\right]
\geq-\varepsilon.
\]
For $\varepsilon<a_\rho$, this contradicts Yokota's second localization (Lemma~\ref{lem:second-localization}) if $x_\varepsilon\notin B(o,\rho)$.
Hence $x_\varepsilon\in B(o,\rho)$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
We work in the normalized case $\kappa=1$; the general case follows by rescaling the metric to $\sqrt{\kappa}\,\mathrm d$.
Fix $\rho\in(r,\pi/2)$.
By Corollary~\ref{cor:localization-approximate-minimizers}, $x_\varepsilon\in B(o,\rho)$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.
The directional derivatives are therefore well defined, and \eqref{eq:ekeland-condition} gives
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:diff-condition}
DF_\mu(x_\varepsilon)[\log_{x_\varepsilon}y]
\geq-\varepsilon\,\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,y),
\qquad y\in B(o,\rho).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

We apply Theorem~\ref{thm:directional-derivative-transfer} to $x_\varepsilon$ and $x_{\varepsilon'}$, using \eqref{eq:diff-condition} in the directions towards $x_{\varepsilon'}$ and $o$.
This yields
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
\label{eq:one-sided-eps}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
F_\mu(x_\varepsilon)-F_\mu(x_{\varepsilon'})
\leq{}&
\varepsilon\Bigl[
\sin\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})
\\
&\quad+
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\bigr)
\tan\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,o)
\Bigr]\\
&-
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\bigr)
\frac{\cos\rho}{\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,o)}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

Interchanging $x_\varepsilon$ and $x_{\varepsilon'}$, we obtain the corresponding inequality with $\varepsilon'$ and $\mathrm d(x_{\varepsilon'},o)$.
Adding the two inequalities and using $\tan\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,o)\leq\tan\rho$, $\tan\mathrm d(x_{\varepsilon'},o)\leq\tan\rho$, and $1/\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,o)\geq1$ (and similarly for $\varepsilon'$), we obtain
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
2\cos\rho\,
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\bigr)
\leq{}&
(\varepsilon+\varepsilon')
\Bigl[\sin\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\\
&+
\bigl(1-\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\bigr)
\tan\rho\Bigr].
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
Since $\sin u\leq1$ and $1-\cos u\leq2$, we obtain
\[
0\leq
1-\cos\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})
\leq
\frac{1+2\tan\rho}{2\cos\rho}
(\varepsilon+\varepsilon')
\longrightarrow0
\]
as $\varepsilon,\varepsilon'\downarrow0$.
Since $\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\leq2\rho<\pi$, it follows that $\mathrm d(x_\varepsilon,x_{\varepsilon'})\to0$.
Thus, $(x_\varepsilon)_{\varepsilon>0}$ is a Cauchy family.
Completeness gives its limit $b(\mu)$.
Since $\rho>r$ was arbitrary, $b(\mu)\in B(o,r)$.
Continuity of $F_\mu$ and \eqref{eq:def-xeps} show that
$F_\mu(b(\mu))=\inf_\mathcal{X} F_\mu$.
Finally, any minimizer belongs to $B(o,r)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:first-localization} and satisfies the first-order condition.
Applying the preceding summed inequality to two minimizers, with $\varepsilon=\varepsilon'=0$, therefore shows that their distance is zero.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Set $A(u):=\int_{C_b\mathcal{X}}\langle u,v\rangle_b\,\mathrm{d}\nu(v)$ for $u\in C_b\mathcal{X}$.
    By the definition of the cone distance, for all $u\in C_b\mathcal{X}$,
    \[
    F_\nu(u)=F_\nu(0_b)+\frac12|u|_b^2-A(u),
    \]
    and the variance inequality
    \cite[Proposition~4.4]{sturm2003} at the barycenter $\beta$
    therefore gives
    \[
    A(u)\leq\langle u,\beta\rangle_b+K,
    \]
    where $K:=F_\nu(0_b)-F_\nu(\beta)-\frac12|\beta|_b^2$ does not depend on $u$.
    Scalar multiplication on $C_b\mathcal{X}$ is defined in Definition~\ref{def:tangent-cone}.
    Applying this inequality to $\lambda u$, dividing by $\lambda>0$, and letting $\lambda\to\infty$, homogeneity yields
    \[
    A(u)\leq\langle u,\beta\rangle_b\leq|u|_b|\beta|_b.
    \]
    Thus $\sup_{|u|_b\leq1}A(u)\leq|\beta|_b$. On the other hand, the same variance inequality, evaluated at $0_b$, gives
    \[
    A(\beta)
    =F_\nu(0_b)-F_\nu(\beta)+\frac12|\beta|_b^2
    \geq|\beta|_b^2.
    \]
    If $\beta\neq0_b$, taking $u=\beta/|\beta|_b$ and using homogeneity proves the reverse inequality. If $\beta=0_b$, equality follows by taking $u=0_b$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
The bound holds for $n=1$ by the definition of $C$.
Suppose that $a_n\leq C/(n+A)$.
Since $n+A\geq\alpha$ and $(\alpha-1)C\geq\beta$,
\[
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
a_{n+1}
&\leq
\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{n+A}\right)\frac{C}{n+A}
+\frac{\beta}{(n+A)^2}\\
&=
\frac{C}{n+A}
-\frac{\alpha C-\beta}{(n+A)^2}\\
&\leq
\frac{C}{n+A}-\frac{C}{(n+A)^2}\\
&\leq
\frac{C}{n+1+A}.
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
\]
This completes the induction.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
